# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [1] [Learning-Theoretic Foundation for General Coded Computing: The Adversarial Setting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27440)
*Parsa Moradi, Hanzaleh Akbarinodehi, Mohammad Ali Maddah-Ali*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Adversarial workers pose a severe challenge in distributed computing systems by returning corrupted outputs that can substantially distort the recovered computations. Coded computing addresses this threat by introducing redundancy across worker assignments. Existing exact and approximate coded-computing methods typically handle adversarial outputs by explicitly locating and correcting or removing erroneous evaluations, which adds significant computational overhead at the master node. Moreover, existing analyses do not characterize how the recovery error scales with the number of workers and the adversarial budget. In this paper, we develop an adversarially robust extension of general coded computing (GCC), a learning-theoretic approximate coded-computing framework that designs the encoder and decoder by minimizing an upper bound on the end-to-end recovery error. Rather than identifying corrupted workers, the proposed method uses smoothness regularization to limit the influence of their outputs. The resulting encoder and decoder can be implemented using smoothing splines with linear master-side complexity. We derive a non-asymptotic bound on the worst-case end-to-end approximation error by decomposing it into three components: (1) the encoder approximation error, (2) the decoder reconstruction error under fully honest results, and (3) the adversarial perturbation error. For maximum of $q\geq 1$ adversarial workers among $N$ workers results in a worst-case error of order $\mathcal{O}((q/N)^{6/5})$ with appropriate selection of smoothness parameter, demonstrating that the approximation error vanishes asymptotically whenever the number of adversaries grows sublinearly with the network size. Experiments on scalar computation and neural-network inference are consistent with the predicted convergence behavior.


### [2] [Unsupervised Head Network Personalization for Split Computing via Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27952)
*Yutaro Horikawa, Takayuki Nishio*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Split Computing (SC) is a distributed inference method designed for balancing computational load and reducing latency by partitioning a neural network into head and tail networks and deploying them across both a mobile device and a server, respectively. Due to the limited bandwidth and computational power of mobile devices, the head network is often replaced with a neural network that requires lower computational and communication resources. However, it is challenging to prepare a sufficient quantity and quality of labeled data on the mobile device side for training the replacement head network. Furthermore, the data prepared by mobile users often contains sensitive privacy information, making data aggregation difficult. In this paper, we propose a data-efficient head network training method for SC. This method utilizes federated learning and knowledge distillation to train the head network using a small amount of non-IID and unlabeled data from mobile devices, ensuring that the training datasets are not exposed. Moreover, by introducing personalization, we enhance the accuracy to levels comparable to or exceeding those of existing methods. The experimental evaluations demonstrate that, despite data constraints imposed only on our method, it achieves accuracy comparable to the baselines. Furthermore, it significantly reduces the communication requirements by 95% on mobile devices, with only a 3-5 percentage point drop in accuracy from the original model.


### [3] [PULSE: Personalized Opportunistic Learning for Wearable Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28081)
*Mattia Giovanni Campana, Dariush Salami, Hüseyin Yiğitler, Stephan Sigg*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Wearable devices increasingly support personalized health and well-being applications, but post-deployment data can differ substantially from those used for training, while purely local adaptation may be limited by the data available on a single device during real-world operation. Conventional federated and decentralized learning rely on recurrent communication and stable connectivity, whereas existing opportunistic approaches often exploit available encounters without adequately considering their utility, compatibility, or communication cost. We propose PULSE, an event-driven framework for personalized opportunistic learning that treats each encounter as an online collaboration decision, selecting either no interaction or a promising nearby peer. Peer gradients are incorporated only when compatible with the local learning direction, while past interactions guide future peer selection. Across human activity recognition, fall detection, and stress recognition under synthetic and real contact traces, PULSE preserves or improves personalized classification performance, while reducing peer-assisted exchanges by 80-94% compared with opportunistic model averaging. On-device experiments further demonstrate its feasibility on wearable hardware: peer-assisted operations require limited energy, while the complete implementation occupies 149.8 KiB of RAM and 561.8 KiB of flash. The obtained results show that selective opportunistic collaboration can provide effective personalization while limiting communication and resource demands on constrained wearable devices.


### [4] [Discount Yourself: Self-inclusive Freshness-weighted Gossip for Decentralised Learning under Client Outages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28545)
*Qiyuan Wang, Ke Xiao, Christos Anagnostopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Decentralised federated learning (DFL) trains personalised models on devices that average with their neighbours rather than with a central server. Real devices, however, are often offline for hours or days, and a client returning from an outage can recover only by aggregating the models its neighbourhood holds. We observe that the stalest of these models is usually the client's own, a case that existing staleness-aware rules, which discount only the models a client receives, do not address. We formalise aggregation after an outage as estimating the state of a fully available network and derive three principles. First, the receiver must be discounted like any neighbour. Every rule that weights it at least as much as each neighbour has an error that grows without bound with the outage, whereas discounting all candidates by relative staleness keeps the error bounded. Second, staleness must be measured as inherited lag rather than age, since local training resets a model's age but not the lag it absorbed from stale neighbours. We introduce the effective lag, which tracks this quantity at the cost of one scalar per message. Third, the discount must be steep, because absorbed lag persists in every later round. These principles define Self-inclusive Freshness-weighted Gossip (SFG), a family of drop-in aggregation rules with the communication and computation of decentralised federated averaging, whose guarantees hold for the stale receiver itself. On three image benchmarks and eight outage processes, including real smartphone availability traces, the lag-based members SFG-L and SFG-Cut consistently outperform decentralised federated averaging and recent age-of-information rules, and ablations show that each principle is necessary. The analysis also explains why tuned limited merging from gossip learning is effective.


### [5] [The Token-Energy Law of LLM Decode: Active Bytes and Realized Time Account for Joules per Token](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28576)
*Chenxu Niu, Batuhan Sencer, Wei Zhang, Yong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Energy-efficient deployment of large language models requires understanding how model architecture, hardware, and serving settings shape energy per generated token. We introduce the Token-Energy Law, a compact, interpretable model of GPU-board energy per output token for dense, full-history decoding. It relates token energy to two core dimensions: active bytes and realized decode time. Active bytes describe logical byte demand per output, separating dense weights shared within a decode iteration from private key--value (KV) histories read and updated for each request. Realized decode time is the elapsed decode duration divided by the number of output tokens produced. We combine the two byte coordinates and time in a common affine form, with coefficients calibrated for each model and hardware/runtime configuration. This formulation explains how batching amortizes shared-weight work, longer attended histories increase private KV demand, and throughput determines the time-associated cost per output. Our experiments cover three models under BF16 decoding on NVIDIA Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, including two-GPU tensor parallelism on Hopper. Using fixed coefficients and observed decode time, held-out condition-mean energy estimates differ from measurements by at most 3.73%.


### [6] [High-dimensional layer-state trajectories reveal the internal dynamics of transformer inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26866)
*Heng Zhao, Yufei Wang, Ruidian Song, Weisheng Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Mechanistic studies of large language models localize function in parameters, attention heads or neurons, but those parts do not specify how one inference unfolds. We treat the ordered layer-state trajectory as the empirical process and test whether layer order defines a measurable computation. Across representative Qwen, Llama and Gemma checkpoints, realized order produced paths 1.60--2.24 times as aligned with their endpoint chord as endpoint-preserving shuffles. The order effect transferred to a lexical task, but was stronger in three random initializations of the same Qwen architecture, identifying an architecture-compatible scaffold rather than a training-specific law. Direct hidden states provided stronger mechanism coordinates than vocabulary readbacks, and a task-defined coordinate made candidate competition computable. After additive interventions failed, local transition actuators selectively controlled internal trajectories across checkpoints. A confidence gate then drove 17 of 87 held-out Qwen closure trajectories across the full-vocabulary top-1 candidate boundary while leaving all 203 non-closure top-1 outputs unchanged. Safety-matched action reassignment performed similarly, locating the gain in selective gating and the bounded actuator family rather than fine-grained dose assignment. Layer order exposes an observable, computable and behaviourally consequential process of transformer inference without establishing a complete theory of LLM dynamics or control of answer correctness.


### [7] [Steerable Pluralistic Policy Alignment from Anonymized Crowd Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26914)
*Akansha Kalra, Ellen R. Novoseller, Vernon J. Lawhern, Daniel S. Brown*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Pluralistic alignment requires AI systems to learn from diverse populations whose preferences may reflect heterogeneous or even conflicting objectives, and to personalize their behavior to individual users. Existing approaches in pluralistic alignment often assume access to annotator identities and rely on powerful pretrained representations to model individual preference variations. We propose Preference-PCA Pluralistic Learning (P3L), a novel framework for pluralistic alignment that jointly learns trajectory embeddings and the shared basis of preference directions via PCA decomposition from heterogeneous crowd feedback without requiring annotator identities. At test time, our approach enables rapid and accurate personalized reward learning and downstream policy optimization. Experiments across multiple embodied control tasks demonstrate that P3L enables steerable personal alignment that generalizes beyond the offline dataset to out-of-distribution new users and attains competitive RL and test performance, while substantially reducing computational cost and supporting variable adaptation query budgets.


### [8] [Expert-Guided Rubrics as Dense Reward Signals for Reinforcement Learning in Non-Verifiable Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26932)
*Ashish Bansal, Hossam Amer, Keerat Kaur Guliani, Parinita Edke, Lesley Matheson, Leizer Teran*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents in non-verifiable domains such as financial services, where they must interpret user intent, rewrite queries, and retrieve information under domain-specific constraints. Although reinforcement learning (RL) can train such agents from the consequences of their actions, recent successes largely rely on verifiable tasks with automatically computed binary rewards. In non-verifiable retrieval tasks, assessing query quality requires scarce subject-matter expert (SME) judgment that is inherently graded rather than binary. With Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), sparse rewards yield no useful learning signal when all sampled outputs within a group receive the same outcome, causing optimization to stall. We introduce a method that converts a small, fixed investment of SME judgment into a dense reward and combines it with retrieval-based metrics to form a hybrid, GRPO-compatible training signal. Experiments show improvements of up to 6\% in Recall@3 and 6.7\% in MRR@3 compared with sparse-reward baselines.


### [9] [Beyond Policy Discrepancy: Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26950)
*Xuanfeng Yang, Le Gui, Jiaming Wang, Xuan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) integrates domain specialists into a shared student, but determining how strongly each teacher should supervise remains challenging. Existing discrepancy-based allocation can assign strong supervision even when the student already matches or exceeds the teacher's task performance, since behavioral mismatch does not necessarily imply a remaining capability deficit. We propose Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (CA-MOPD), which combines specialist merging with capability-guided allocation to account for what the student has already learned. CA-MOPD first merges reinforcement-learning specialists into a strong student initialization, then uses verifier-estimated teacher--student capability gaps to adjust domain-level distillation strength. Supervision decreases as capability gaps close and increases when estimated student capability regresses, while dense token-level guidance remains provided by the teachers. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and instruction following, CA-MOPD achieves an average score of 32.22, outperforming naive MOPD, Open-MOPD, and the merged initialization by 0.86, 0.83, and 0.74 points, respectively. Controlled comparisons further support the benefits of merged initialization and capability-gap guidance under matched training budgets.


### [10] [Dynamic System Inspired Continuous Large Language Model for AI4Science Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26959)
*Zhaoxin Fan, Zheng Zhu, Yuqing Ma, Faguo Wu, Jian Yang, Wenjun Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scientific foundation models increasingly operate on continuous phenomena, yet still use discrete residual computation. We ask whether pretrained residual transformations can support additional latent computation without expanding the backbone. To test this possibility, we introduce Continuum-LM, which reuses these transformations as dynamical operators in a continuous-depth language model. Its hidden states evolve through an ordinary differential equation evaluated by higher-order numerical integration, with solver order and step size controlling computation. Across eight scientific tasks, Continuum-LM trains stably and improves mean exact-match accuracy by 7.77 and 10.96 percentage points over discrete counterparts at the 0.6B and 1.7B scales, respectively. At 0.6B, it also outperforms an Ouro-style recurrent control under matched parameter and residual-function evaluation budgets. Together, these results support extensible computational depth through parameter reuse, identifying continuous hidden-state evolution as a practical design dimension for scientific language models beyond parameter scaling.


### [11] [Transformers Learn Two Competing Sorting Algorithms, and Only One Length-Generalizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26974)
*Khashayar Gatmiry, Nathan W. Henry, Chloe H. Su, Emmanuel Abbe, Samy Bengio, Melanie Weber, Jonathan A. Kelner, Peter L. Bartlett*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We show that two-layer transformers trained to near-zero loss on a sorting task converge to one of two mechanistically distinct solution classes depending on their initialization. One solution class consists of solutions that do not use the second attention layer to sort, but also fail to length-generalize. The other, which uses both attention layers, length-generalizes perfectly, despite only being trained to sort sequences of a fixed length. By carefully choosing the weight initialization, we demonstrate that we can steer the model toward learning one solution or the other. We identify input types that are rare at the training length but increasingly common at longer sequence lengths as a source of this disparity in length-generalization, and find that layer-wise specialization of the second class of solutions allows them to handle these rare sequences more accurately. In the second class, we discover a mechanism by which the second layer attention can use the contextual information gathered by the first layer attention to reduce its search space. On the related task of finding the minimum of a sequence, we find a similar two solution-type phenomenon, but with the opposite generalization pattern, where a different form of layer-wise specialization causes the two-layer solution to length-generalize worse than the single-attention solution.


### [12] [Latent Projection for Latent Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26979)
*Yoo Sung Jang, Michael Ryoo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Latent world models let an agent plan in latent space. The planner searches for an action sequence that minimizes a cost, and that cost is often the $\ell_2$ distance between the predicted terminal latent and the goal latent. The latent, however, is partly noise: the regularizer that prevents collapse during world model training promotes variance in the directions carrying no signal. We show that the same regularizer also supplies the structure to prune that noise in the latent. SIGReg drives the latent marginal to a standard Gaussian at every step, and assuming further that the latent sequence is a Gauss-Markov process, the transition is linear with its covariance pinned by the map itself, $z' \mid z \sim \mathcal{N}(Az,\, I - AA^\top)$. Fitting the state dynamics $A$ therefore yields the covariance at no additional cost, and in its eigenbasis each latent direction carries its own uncertainty. Furthermore, constraining $A$ to be a normal matrix fixes that basis across timesteps, so one eigenbasis measures the uncertainty over an arbitrary number of steps and serves as a stable basis to compute the planning cost on. We then restrict the $\ell_2$ cost to the $k$-dimensional subspace the state dynamics carry forward with the least uncertainty. The correction is post hoc: the encoder, the predictor, the rollout, and the planner are all left untouched, and only a single constrained matrix, $A$, is fit. We evaluate across six environments and show large gains on the 2D navigation tasks, TwoRoom and PointMaze, raising success rate over the plain $\ell_2$ cost by up to $+54.7$ points under a harder planning setting. Code is available at this https URL .


### [13] [CoASFL: Coupled Consistency Optimization for Asynchronous Split Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26992)
*Junhao Cheng, Yunming Liao, Hongli Xu, Yang Xu, Zuan Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Split Federated Learning (SFL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for training large-scale deep models on resource-constrained edge devices. Although asynchronous SFL alleviates the straggler bottleneck caused by system heterogeneity, it inevitably introduces two coupled dimensions of inconsistency: horizontal inconsistency across clients and vertical inconsistency between clients and the server. These inconsistencies, further exacerbated by statistical heterogeneity, result in biased activation representations, model divergence, and severe client drift. To address these challenges, we propose CoASFL, an inconsistency-aware asynchronous SFL framework that incorporates a dynamic activation merging mechanism and a consistency-aware model aggregation mechanism. Specifically, CoASFL constructs more consistent activation sequences from heterogeneous clients to alleviate horizontal inconsistency, and dynamically adjusts aggregation weights according to client participation frequencies and progress gaps to mitigate vertical inconsistency. By jointly coordinating activation-level and model-level consistency, CoASFL effectively suppresses inconsistency propagation during asynchronous training. Extensive experiments on an 80-device heterogeneous edge testbed demonstrate that CoASFL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art SFL approaches, accelerating training by 1.61$\times$ to 3.74$\times$ and improving final model accuracy by up to 3.26\% under heterogeneous scenarios.


### [14] [CorteXforge: Scenario-Based RF Dataset Generation on Real Testbeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26999)
*Andréa Joly, Leonardo S Cardoso, Jean-Marie Gorce*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep learning for radio frequency (RF) signal analysis relies on large annotated datasets, yet synthetic datasets trade physical realism for controllability, while uncontrolled over-theair measurements offer realism but limited reproducibility. We present CorteXforge, a scenario-based framework for generating realistic and reproducible RF datasets on programmable software defined radio (SDR) testbeds. High-level RF scenarios are automatically translated into synchronized experiments executed on the SLICES-RI/CorteXlab platform, producing fully annotated Signal Metadata Format (SIGMF) datasets that capture hardware impairments and propagation effects. We validate CorteXforge through automatic modulation classification using ModForge, an over-the-air (OTA) dataset generated with the framework. Cross-domain experiments reveal a synthetic-to-real performance gap and show that synthetic pretraining remains beneficial for adaptation to OTA data. CorteXforge provides a practical foundation for reproducible RF dataset generation and future research on domain generalization.


### [15] [ORBIT: Autoregressive Time Series Generation via Next Frequency-Band Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27000)
*Chunyi Hou, Xiangfei Qiu, Hanyin Cheng, Chenchen Sun, Yisheng An, Yang Shu, Jilin Hu, Bin Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep generative models provide an effective modern solution to alleviating the problem of data scarcity by synthesizing realistic time series. However, despite the considerable progress achieved by existing deep time series generators, two key challenges remain: (1) learning hierarchical representations for heterogeneous temporal patterns; and (2) coordinating the generation process of heterogeneous temporal dynamics. To address these key bottlenecks, in this work, we propose ORBIT, a unified deep generative framework for time series. Specifically, we develop two key components: the Frequency-Hierarchical VQ-VAE (FH-VQ-VAE) and the Frequency-Band Autoregressive Transformer (FBAR). The former learns hierarchical representations by decomposing latent temporal representations into different frequency bands and applying residual quantization with increasing token budgets. The latter establishes a novel low-to-high frequency autoregressive direction, yielding a time series generation process that first constructs global temporal structures and then incrementally incorporates fine-grained temporal details. These compact designs not only disentangle heterogeneous temporal patterns for more effective representation learning but also mitigate potential interference among different temporal dynamics during generation. Extensive experiments on multiple challenging real-world benchmarks demonstrate that ORBIT achieves state-of-the-art overall performance while exhibiting superior inference efficiency compared with advanced baselines.


### [16] [HierDTA: Hierarchical Structure-Aware Representation Learning for Drug--Target Affinity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27001)
*Huangchao Xu, Baohua Zhang, Qian Liu, Zhong Jin, Yi Du*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Drug--target affinity (DTA) prediction plays an important role in computer-aided drug discovery, where reliable computational models can help prioritize compound--protein pairs before experimental validation. A key challenge in DTA prediction is to effectively mine multi-level molecular information from drug structures while accurately modeling the binding-site environment of target proteins. To address these issues, we introduce HierDTA, a hierarchical structure-aware framework for DTA prediction. \textcolor{black}{HierDTA represents each drug as an atom--motif heterogeneous graph and introduces an augmentation module that propagates functional-motif semantics to the corresponding atoms. For proteins, kinase-domain structures are converted into residue interaction graphs. The resulting atom and residue representations are integrated using cross-attention to capture pair-specific relationships, while molecule fingerprints and pretrained ESM-2 embeddings provide complementary global information. Compared with state-of-the-art methods, HierDTA achieves superior performance on the Davis dataset across all metrics and attains the highest \(r_m^2\) of 0.791 on KIBA. It also exhibits strong generalization under cold-start settings, particularly on KIBA, where MSE is reduced by 21.7\% in the unseen-protein scenario. Ablation study and interpretability analysis supported the contributions of the hierarchical augmentation and interaction components.} Case studies further showed that HierDTA provided early enrichment of candidate bioactive compounds. These results indicate that HierDTA is an effective framework for affinity prediction and target-specific virtual screening.


### [17] [Exploration via Activation-Space Steering for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27012)
*Yunan Zeng, Jinhao Jing, Xi Cheng, Yuwang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains a language model by sampling responses, scoring them with an automatic verifier, and updating the policy from the resulting rewards. The policy therefore receives direct learning signals only from trajectories that appear in its rollouts. Yet repeated sampling from the same policy can concentrate on similar reasoning paths, leaving useful alternatives unexplored under a fixed rollout budget. We introduce Activation-Space Exploration (ASE), an online method for diversifying training rollouts. By clustering activation differences between correct and incorrect responses to the same problem, ASE builds a structured bank of latent exploration vectors. Different vectors are then applied to subsequent rollouts to explore alternative reasoning paths. On Qwen3-1.7B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base trained on DAPO-Math-17k, ASE improves average Mean@8 across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks over GRPO by 4.87 and 1.87 percentage points, respectively, under the same rollout budget.


### [18] [EGiME: Entropy-Guided Model Evaluation in Few-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27029)
*Dequan Jin, Tao Li, Lifeng Xing, Shihui Ying, Hui Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The feature-extraction quality of pre-trained models sets the ceiling of downstream transfer learning, yet few-shot model selection remains difficult: supervised evaluation on scarce labels is unstable and yields fluctuating rankings. We propose Entropy-Guided Model Evaluation (EGiME), a semi-supervised criterion that assesses a candidate model from the clustering structure of its feature space without any classifier training. EGiME performs $K$-means clustering over all labeled and unlabeled samples, then combines two entropy terms: an inter-cluster entropy measuring global separability, and an intra-cluster entropy measuring the compactness of labeled anchors, fused through an inverse-frequency weight that emphasizes small clusters. On five few-shot benchmarks and nine pre-trained models, EGiME achieves a correlation score of 0.966 with the full-pool ground-truth accuracy obtained by averaging the accuracies of K-means, KNN, and SVM. At labeling ratios of 4\%--20\%, it produces markedly more stable model rankings than cross-validated supervised baselines, establishing it as a practical alternative to conventional supervised evaluation for pre-trained model selection under data scarcity.


### [19] [Hilbert-Scale Stability of Posterior Diffusion Priors for Ill-Posed Signal Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27030)
*Bahman Moraffah, Raha Moraffah*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Indirect and noisy observations are difficult to invert when the forward operator smooths out the unknown signal. In this case, the unstable modes are precisely the modes that the data reveals least, thus the success of any reconstruction method depends on how prior information interacts with the geometry of the operator across regularity scales. Diffusion priors have become ubiquitous for such problems; however, the current theory still does not determine when a learned score remains informative under ill-posed forward operators. In this paper, we study posterior diffusion for linear inverse problems on Hilbert scales and derive a stability theory under an explicit score-amplification condition. Under a smoothing condition on the forward map, a source condition on the truth, and weak-norm control of score error, we derive non-asymptotic recovery bounds in \(\Hs^s\) that separate observation noise, score approximation, and reverse-time discretization. These bounds give a recoverable regularity threshold determined jointly by operator smoothing, truth regularity, score regularity, and the posterior correction. The threshold specifies the Hilbert-scale norms \(\Hs^s\) in which weak-norm score error remains controlled after propagation through the posterior reverse dynamics. For larger regularity indices \(s\), the inverse geometry magnifies the same error, and the Hilbert-scale recovery estimate no longer gives uniform stability. We also prove that a posterior correction adapted to the forward operator improves stability relative to isotropic guidance in the regularity ranges where the smoothing action of the forward map matters most. The recovery estimates give a regularity-sensitive theory of posterior diffusion and show how learned score error, operator smoothing, and reconstruction norm jointly determine the stable range predicted by the analysis.


### [20] [LEAF: LUT-aware Efficient Attention on FPGAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27078)
*Arianna Cox, Chang Sun, Lauri Laatu, Benedikt Maier, Aadeesh Sharma, Leo Rozanov, Wayne Luk, Maria Spiropulu, Alexander Tapper, Zhiqiang Que*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce Lookup-table (LUT)-aware Efficient Attention on FPGAs (LEAF). LEAF is the first nanosecond-scale LUT-aware transformer for FPGAs. Deploying transformers that are large enough to perform well, small enough to fit on an FPGA, and fast enough to meet the sub-microsecond timescales required by real-time systems such as those at CERN remains difficult, since the same attention mechanism that gives these models their high expressivity also makes them costly to implement in hardware. LEAF converts the query, key, and value projections and the final output feed-forward layer into FPGA-native layers with LUT-aware training, creating the first LUT-aware transformer and linformer layers built for ultra-low-latency inference. Attention resists LUT-aware training because it multiplies two activations where the method needs a constant weight to tabulate; LEAF tabulates the attention block's constant matrix-vector multiplications and leaves the rest in arithmetic form. On a standard jet tagging benchmark this improves accuracy by 1.8 percentage points while using 2.9 times fewer LUTs than the previous state-of-the-art sub-microsecond transformer at the same input size. Our most efficient LEAF-based linformer with 64 input particles reaches 80.4% classification accuracy at 28k LUTs and 35ns.


### [21] [ClockMark: Adaptive and Host-Dependent Watermarking for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27079)
*Yupeng Qiu, Han Fang, Ee-Chien Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) watermarking operates on the next-token distribution that evolves during generation. In streaming generation, each sampled token becomes part of the text and affects subsequent distributions. Embedding therefore proceeds with a changing host, unlike classical watermarking, which adapts embedding to an existing host signal. To accommodate this difference, we formulate a novel embedding framework for LLM watermarking based on two principles. We first argue that embedding should be adaptive, retaining the state of previous sampling decisions to better guide subsequent choices. We further argue that embedding should be host-dependent, using the current LLM distribution to constrain both token selection and state updates. Guided by these principles and the two requirements of distribution preservation and robustness to text editing, we propose ClockMark. It combines adaptive and host-dependent embedding with message spreading, embedding the full payload in each watermarked token. Theoretically, we prove that ClockMark preserves the original LLM sequence distribution, thereby maintaining generation quality. Empirically, extensive experiments against 12 baselines demonstrate improved robustness to text editing, with gains of up to 16.08 percentage points in bit accuracy over the strongest tested baseline.


### [22] [RGH-GNN : Risk-Gated Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network for Financial Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27107)
*Mythreyee Hari, Subin Sahayam, Vegesna S M Srinivasavarma*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Credit card fraud is a significant problem for financial institutions, resulting in substantial monetary loss each year. Fraud patterns and methods are continuously evolving, with fraudulent transactions often disguised to resemble legitimate activity. Real world transaction data for fraud detection is severely affected by class imbalance making it harder to detect these transactions. Fraudulent transactions are rarely isolated. Accounts, devices, and other common attributes are often shared across fraudulent activity, revealing relational patterns that individual transaction features alone cannot capture. Graph-based approaches model these relationships explicitly. However, standard graph neural networks aggregate neighbourhood information uniformly, which can dilute the already scarce signal carried by fraudulent transactions and limit detection of camouflaged fraud. This work presents RGH-GNN, a heterogeneous graph neural network that addresses this limitation by incorporating transaction-level risk scores, generated by a supervised LightGBM classifier as edge weights. It is used to guide message passing through a learnable risk-gated aggregation mechanism. It ensures that high-risk transactions contribute more strongly to learned representations rather than being diluted by uniform neighbourhood aggregation. RGH-GNN is evaluated on the IEEE-CIS fraud detection dataset against standard graph neural network models that are trained on the same heterogeneous graph. RGH-GNN outperforms all baselines across every evaluation metric, with an AUC-ROC score of 0.914. This demonstrates that incorporating engineered risk signals into graph-based message passing substantially improves fraud detection performance.


### [23] [Reconstruction-Optimized Expert Pruning for Mixture-of-Experts Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27123)
*Jelena Markovic-Voronov, Petros Prastakos, Kayhan Behdin, Jincheng Cao, Zhipeng Wang, Shayan Mohajer Hamidi, Yuanda Xu, Zhengze Zhou, Wenhui Zhu, Rahul Mazumder*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models reduce computation through sparse expert activation, but deployment still requires storing a large number of expert parameters. We study post-training expert pruning using task-domain calibration data as a subset-selection problem: given a pretrained MoE layer, select a fixed number of experts that best reconstruct its original output. Exact subset search is combinatorial and becomes intractable for modern MoE models with many experts per layer. We develop OptEP, a scalable continuous relaxation that directly optimizes differentiable expert-selection variables, followed by a lightweight reconstruction-based update of the retained expert weights. Across multiple MoE architectures and tasks spanning mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approach improves over the strongest existing expert-pruning baselines, with absolute accuracy gains of up to 15.05% on Qwen3-30B-A3B, 14.55% on Qwen3-235B-A22B, 11.06% on GLM-4.5-Air, and 16.20% on Mixtral-8x7B. The improvements are particularly pronounced under aggressive pruning, demonstrating that reconstruction-driven expert selection and weight adaptation can preserve substantially more of the original model's performance while reducing the number of retained experts.


### [24] [Glass-Box Deep Reinforcement Learning for High-Speed Flow Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27132)
*Ameya D. Jagtap*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) is challenging long-held assumptions in active flow control. By learning directly from interaction with a flow, DRL agents can find actuation strategies for nonlinear, unsteady flows without explicit models, often exploiting mechanisms that classical control would not anticipate. High-speed flows are a demanding test of this promise: shocks, shock/boundary-layer interactions, transition, aerothermal loading and microsecond-scale dynamics strain both algorithms and hardware. Yet most demonstrations remain idealized, low-speed simulations with limited evidence of robustness, interpretability, transferability or experimental deployment. In this perspective, we examine what constrains DRL-based high-speed flow control, including compressible simulation cost and fidelity, data efficiency, sensing in extreme environments, reward design, explainability, and generalization across Mach and Reynolds number. We argue that these limits reflect a tension between data-driven optimization and the structured physics, constraints and uncertainties of real flows. We then highlight where DRL may go beyond conventional approaches: adaptive control of shock-induced separation and turbulent boundary layers, coordination of distributed actuators, and discovery of unconventional flow-manipulation this http URL central challenge, however, is translating these capabilities into reliable technology, which will require validated compressible simulation environments, physics- and constraint-aware learning, robustness to off-design conditions and sensor degradation, real-time operation, hardware-in-the-loop and wind-tunnel validation, and credible pathways to safety and certification.


### [25] [Ontic state recovery through prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27139)
*Ping Zhang, Quang-Huy Nguyen, Yingtie Lei, Wei-Lun Chao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We encounter a system through its responses, yet its state concerns how the system is. Predicting further responses promises knowledge beyond what is immediately observed. What that knowledge tells us about the system's state depends on what we mean by a state in the first place. We give this notion a model-relative meaning: a state is the complete response profile over a specified query universe, and configurations agreeing on every query are regarded as the same state. From this perspective, a learned state is understood through the distinctions between these profiles that it retains. Prediction concerns selected observations; recovery concerns the profile as a whole. We examine their relationship through query ambiguity, state-space geometry, and the choice of prediction targets. Synthetic systems with known response laws and neural recordings provide complementary settings for this investigation. The aim is to clarify how prediction can become a way of knowing a system, and what it would mean for a learned state to preserve the state being studied.


### [26] [Representing short-term movement to reveal animal identity and learning progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27140)
*Ping Zhang, Quang-Huy Nguyen, Yingtie Lei, Sameera Shridhar, Zachary Leveroni, Ellie Quattrocchi, Jai Y Yu, Wei-Lun Chao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: How learning progresses over time and varies across individuals is a longstanding question in cognitive and behavioral science. However, a major challenge is identifying observable behavioral measures that faithfully capture learning progression. Existing approaches commonly rely on predefined behavioral features or task-level performance, while fine-scale posture and movement in freely behaving animals remain less explored. In this paper, we ask: can short-term movement trajectories reveal learning progress and individual identity? We introduce a self-supervised representation learning framework built on a simple hypothesis: behavioral characteristics associated with identity and learning remain relatively stable over nearby time intervals. Using temporal proximity as supervision, our framework learns compact representations directly from movement trajectories without manually defining behavioral states or kinematic features. Using behavior data from freely behaving rats, we show that the learned representations not only improve prediction of identity and learning-related readouts over existing methods, but also provide a tool for investigating broader scientific questions about when and how these signals appear in behavior. Specifically, trajectories as short as one second contain informative learning and identity signals. Egocentric pose dynamics are more predictive of identity, whereas allocentric motion is more predictive of learning-related variation, and both signals are strongest at decision junctions in the alternation cohort. We further use a representation-conditioned diffusion model to inspect movement patterns associated with identity and learning progress. Our findings establish short-term movement as an informative behavioral "fingerprint" for studying learning and individual differences. Our code is available at this https URL .


### [27] [A Hybrid TFT-NBEATS Framework for Robust Multi-Horizon Photovoltaic Power Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27155)
*Shafi Bin Sultan, Sabik Bin Sultan, Mohammad Tajrian Arafat Alvi, Rayhanul Islam Sony, Abdulah Al Numan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Accurate multi-horizon photovoltaic (PV) power forecasting is essential for grid stability, energy scheduling, and the efficient operation of PVbatterygrid systems. However, existing models often fail to jointly capture short-term variability and long-term temporal patterns, while lacking unified multi-horizon capability and interpretability. To address these limitations, this study proposes a novel hybrid deep learning framework that integrates a Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) and N-BEATS within an attention-based fusion architecture. The proposed model leverages TFT to learn short-term temporal dependencies and dynamic feature importance, while N-BEATS captures long-term trend and seasonal components through interpretable basis decomposition. An adaptive attention mechanism dynamically fuses both branches, enabling balanced multi-scale learning. The framework simultaneously generates forecasts for 30-minute, 2-hour, 6-hour, 12-hour, and 24-hour horizons within a single unified pipeline. The model is evaluated using multi-year real-world datasets from the Desert Knowledge Australia Solar Centre (DKASC). Experimental results demonstrate superior performance over statistical, machine learning, and deep learning baselines. The proposed model achieves RMSE values of 42.35, 46.27, 52.29, and 48.11 across the 20162017, 2023, 2024, and 20252026 datasets, respectively, corresponding to a 3540% reduction compared to the best baseline (TFT). Additionally, MAPE remains below 5% across all datasets, indicating high predictive accuracy and stability. The model also provides interpretability through attention visualization and trendseasonality decomposition, and is deployed on a cloud-based platform for real-time forecasting. These results demonstrate that the proposed framework is robust, interpretable, and suitable for practical renewable energy applications.


### [28] [Understanding the Benefits of Scaffolds through Computational Separations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27157)
*Jingchu Gai, Adam Block, Andrej Risteski*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Inference-time harnesses, i.e., external procedures that call a model many times and keep state across calls, can substantially improve model capabilities. In this paper, we investigate the benefits of using such harnesses and the extent to which these benefits can be internalized through training. More precisely, we conceptualize a harness $\mathcal{A}$ as an arbitrary wrapper around a base model $M$ and attempt to distill a student model from the harness's traces. Empirically we find that, even with simple scaffolds and small models, the inference-time harness significantly outperforms a distilled student. We then prove that such a gap is due to a fundamental representational limitation: inference-time harnesses can compute a closure of their per-call primitive that is $\NL$-complete even when the primitive is a trivial $\AC^0$ lookup, and that no fixed-depth forward pass can represent under the widely believed conjecture that $\NL\not\subseteq\TC^0$. Our theory suggests that expanding the representational capacity of the student relative to the task at hand, e.g., by enlarging the model, making the task easier, or lengthening its chain-of-thought, is necessary to close the gap, which we verify empirically. Finally, we scale to an agentic harness on competition math, where the same gap reappears. Overall, our results show that a harness confers on the base model a profound gain in representational power, one that training a same-class student on its traces cannot transfer back into the weights.


### [29] [Meta-iLaD: Identifiable Latent Dynamics via Meta-Learning of Dynamics Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27180)
*Yubo Ye, Sweekar Piya, Xiajun Jiang, Linwei Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Learning latent dynamics is central to assessing the current states and forecasting the future trajectories of high-dimensional time series. For locally-stationary latent dynamics $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{z}_{<t}; \mathbf{c})$ with latent dynamics state $\mathbf{z}_t$ and environment variable $\mathbf{c}$, prior identifiability results have largely focused on $\mathbf{z}_t$ when conditioned on predefined label $u$ of dynamics environments. This leaves two limitations: reliance on predefined labels that hinder generalization to unseen environments, and a limited understanding of the identifiability of $\mathcal{F}$ and $\mathbf{c}$ which -- although offering important structural properties for the identifiability of $\mathbf{z}_t$ -- are learned jointly with $\mathbf{z}_t$. We address these limitations with Meta-iLaD, a novel identifiable latent dynamics framework attained by meta-learning across dynamics environments. Meta-iLaD introduces a novel conditional prior of $\mathbf{c}$, modeled as a feedforward meta-learner to rapidly extract $\mathbf{c}$ from few-shot examples. Meta-iLaD further establishes identifiability for $\mathbf{z}_t$, $\mathbf{c}$, and $\mathcal{F}$ simultaneously, for a general formulation of $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{z}_{< t};\mathbf{c})$ without restricting the dimension of $\mathbf{c}$ or how it modulates $\mathcal{F}$. On synthetic and real data, we provide strong empirical evidence that 1) conditioning on few-shot examples enables generalization to out-of-distribution environments, and 2) identifiability for $\mathbf{c}$ and $\mathcal{F}$ is critical for accurate forecasting beyond reconstructing observed trajectories. Code is available at this https URL .


### [30] [You Are the Trigger: Speaker-Activated Backdoors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27210)
*Nataša Krčo, Euodia Dodd, Peter Romov, Yves-Alexandre de Montjoye*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Speech models are increasingly used as an interface to AI systems, from transcription tools to voice agents. Existing audio backdoors require an attacker to introduce triggers at inference time. We study a different threat model in which the attacker compromises the model during fine-tuning but has no access to the victim's inference-time inputs. We introduce the Speaker-Activated Backdoor (SAB) setting, in which a target speaker's natural voice serves as the trigger. We develop a two-phase fine-tuning method that first makes speaker identity more accessible and then binds the target speaker to an attacker-chosen payload. Compared with a one-phase baseline with the same training budget, the two-phase method substantially improves selectivity and clean performance, and is especially important for content-dependent attacks. SAB achieves 90.2% and 97.0% backdoor success on Whisper-medium and Parakeet, with false-positive rates of 1.7% and 0%, respectively using as little as two minutes of real audio from the target speaker. We further demonstrate content-dependent semantic rewriting and downstream tool-use attacks, where malicious instructions are inserted into 96.9% of target-speaker transcripts. When these transcripts are passed to frontier LLM agents, GPT-4o and Claude (Opus, Sonnet, and Haiku), the injected instruction drives the agent to carry out the harmful action in up to 87.5% of cases. The attack generalizes to held-out recordings, multiple target speakers, and common acoustic degradations. These results establish speaker identity as a practical backdoor trigger that requires no inference-time attacker access and is invisible at the transcript layer.


### [31] [A Theoretical Analysis of Environment-Aware Out-of-Distribution Detection for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27211)
*Salimeh Sekeh, Mary Wisell*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continual vision-language models (VLMs) encounter a stream of environments and must also reject inputs that do not belong to the environment at hand. Class information from earlier environments can aid out-of-distribution (OOD) detection, yet existing detectors score each environment in isolation, and when previous environments help or hurt is theoretically unclear. We develop a theory of environment-aware OOD detection built on contribution scores, pairwise cosine expectations measuring how the image and text features of one environment align with those of another. We propose a cross-environment maximum-concept-matching (MCM) score with two terms: a primary term matching the test image against the current class texts, and a secondary term matching it against the class texts of a previous environment, weighted by the intra-modal alignment between the two environments. We prove that, under a positive environment transition and a bounded class-matching margin for OOD inputs, this score attains a false-positive rate no larger than that of the standard MCM detector at an adjusted threshold. We then show that the score gap of an MCM detector between two environments equals the self-contribution of the in-distribution environment minus its cross-modal contribution into the OOD environment, which separates soft OOD environments, with explicit AUROC, true-positive, and false-positive guarantees, from hard ones, which no threshold that retains the in-distribution data can reliably reject. For cascaded detection, in which the text prototypes of all seen environments are pooled, the false-positive rate on every past environment is non-decreasing in time, and we characterize when detection provably fails. A contribution balance factor applied to the gradients of the classification loss and of a smoothed false-positive rate completes the framework.


### [32] [Deep catastrophic-risk-aware reinforcement learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27214)
*Carlos Pérez Octavio Mendoza, Frédéric Godin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Estimating extreme risk in reinforcement learning becomes increasingly difficult as the confidence level increases, since only a small number of observations contribute to empirical estimates of Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR). In this paper, we extend an Extreme Value Theory (EVT) approach for CVaR optimization to high-dimensional neural-network policies. Using a peaks-over-threshold representation and method-of-moments estimation, we obtain a differentiable tail-risk objective and show that, conditional on the selected threshold, its finite-sample gradient can be computed directly through automatic differentiation. We first validate the approach in a controlled experiment with a known optimal solution and then investigate its performance in a deep-hedging problem with rare extreme market movements. Our results show similar hedging behavior for EVT- and empirical-CVaR-trained policies under normal conditions, while EVT-trained policies exhibit lower hedging losses under the extreme-scenario evaluation across the training sample sizes considered. These results suggest that EVT can provide a practical alternative for training neural policies when the observations relevant to extreme risk are scarce.


### [33] [Solving the Ho--Nguyen Conjecture for Gaussian Mixtures: Applications to Mixtures of Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27265)
*Yuyang Zhang, Qihuang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Over-fitted Gaussian mixtures can contain many parameter configurations with nearly the same density, making the order of density cancellation central to their parameter-learning rates. Ho and Nguyen encoded these cancellations in a polynomial system and conjectured its exact order as a function of cluster size. The key to resolving the conjecture is a common positive-variance shift that turns the coefficient equations into Gaussian moment matching. For every cluster size $\Mhn\ge2$, we prove that the real, nonzero-weight system has order $2\Mhn$: a sharp sign-change bound forces rigidity at that order, while positive Gauss--Hermite quadrature constructs a non-trivial solution through order $2\Mhn-1$. Population-density calculations at 110-digit working precision recover the predicted leading term for two to six components; finite multistart searches recover the same observed powers for two to five components, with smaller finite-scale constants for three to five components. For parameter learning, the exact order gives an explicit pointwise maximum-likelihood exponent and an exact local minimax order on specified shrinking neighbourhoods of a fixed known mixing measure. The same coefficient viewpoint separates high-order cancellation from exact parameter symmetries in mixtures of experts. Synchronized replication preserves the conditional density while keeping the corresponding parameter losses positive in two broad standard-softmax classes, showing why statistical rate arguments must account for exact parameter symmetries.


### [34] [VPFlow: Value-Predictive Planning with Imperfect World Models for Offline Goal Reaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27280)
*Yunzhe Hu, Dong Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning enables skill acquisition by reaching any goal from any state without dense rewards. Model-based methods allow for planning by scaling test-time compute to solve complex tasks. Combining both holds appealing promise for pre-training on suboptimal demonstrations and zero-shot adaptation via goal specification and planning. Yet existing methods assume effective planning requires accurate next-state prediction in pixel or latent space. In this work, we show that value prediction is more useful for planning, whereas accurate state prediction is often unnecessary. Our key insight is that offline goal reaching naturally admits a closed-form Monte Carlo return, enabling value regression to replace temporal-difference bootstrapping. We use this return as the sole supervision for latent world models: imagined latents are trained to predict goal-reaching value rather than match future states, implicitly preventing representation collapse. These planning-oriented representations let imperfect world models imagine on the level sets of value field beyond dataset transitions. We then present VPFlow, a value-predictive planner that pairs the world models with a one-step flow policy and plans in its noise space via evolutionary search. It enables policy improvement with imagined generalized advantage and reduces out-of-distribution queries common in action-space planning. On 30 state and pixel OGBench datasets, VPFlow surpasses model-free baselines by at least 30.3 and 10.1 percentage points in success rate, respectively, and outperforms latent planners LeWorldModel, PLDM, and TD-MPC2. It achieves this despite a latent prediction error orders of magnitude larger than with state prediction, while minimizing this error lowers success rate. This demonstrates that value-predictive representations, not exact state prediction, are what offline planning requires.


### [35] [VampPrior Unleashed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27322)
*Andrew A. Stirn, Abhejit Rajagopal, David A. Knowles, Grace Huynh*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep latent variable models (DLVMs) underpin many modern analysis pipelines, for example in single-cell genomics, yet their prior distributions have received markedly little attention. The VampPrior approximates the optimal prior (the aggregate posterior) and often attains high model evidence. Here, we identify two key limitations of the VampPrior: it ties prior and posterior uncertainty to a single amortized covariance and clusters poorly due to its fixed uniform mixture weights. We unleash the VampPrior by decoupling its prior and posterior covariances, resolving the conflict of generating high prior and low posterior entropy with the same shared parameters and substantially improving model evidence. We then replace the uniform mixture with a Dirichlet process. When combined, our proposals provide higher model evidence than the VampPrior and robust non-parametric clustering. Augmenting single cell Variational Inference (scVI) with our prior improves single-cell integration and clustering across six atlases while significantly raising model evidence. Our approach changes only the prior and thus can be applied to any DLVM with a continuous latent space.


### [36] [Automated Brick Schema Knowledge Graph Completion Across Buildings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27339)
*Daniel Ma, Jalil Huseynov, Xinyi Pan, Shaked Shalev, Zhang Chi, Ai Yamamoto, Sohei Arisaka*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The Brick schema represents building equipment, sensors, spaces, and the relationships between them as typed, multi-relational graphs, providing machine-readable metadata that supports generalizable control applications. Current methods for producing complete Brick models require extensive manual examination of building schematics and point lists. We observe that as Brick models are knowledge graphs, inferring missing relations is naturally posed as a knowledge graph completion problem. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first application of knowledge graph completion to Brick model generation. We compare three methods, gradient-boosted trees, Pairwise Brick Relation Ranking, and CompGCN, on the relations feeds, hasPart, and hasPoint, which account for the majority of the schematic-reading work in building a Brick model by hand. Models are evaluated on held-out MORTAR buildings and out-of-distribution Kaggle and Building Time Series data. Although CompGCN is the strongest performer on held-out buildings under metrics resistant to class imbalance, leading macro-F1 and AUPRC, no method generalizes uniformly across datasets or relations.


### [37] [LiBRA: Logit Fingerprints for Byzantine-Robust Client Selection in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27357)
*Saheed A. Tijani, Frank Jiang, Ming Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Byzantine-robust aggregation shields federated learning from malicious updates, but the rules that filter out suspect updates do so by comparing clients' parameter vectors. That comparison requires access to every client's update, which secure aggregation exists to withhold, costs $O(n^2 d)$ in the parameter dimension $d$, and is defined only between clients that share an architecture. We propose LiBRA, which compares clients on what their models compute rather than on the parameters that compute them, using a fingerprint of $C^2$ values for $C$ classes, the class-conditional logit response of each client's model to pseudo-patterns optimised against it. We instantiate LiBRA as variants of Krum, Multi-Krum and Bulyan that change only what the dissimilarity score is computed on. On Fashion-MNIST and CIFAR-10 with three architectures and four backdoor and label-manipulation attacks under i.i.d. partitions, each variant holds every attack its parent holds, and on identical client updates under a backdoor attack the fingerprint ranks malicious clients within 0.006 AUC of the full parameter vector. The variants thus match the robustness of the rules they extend, while their trust decision reads no client parameter, compares up to 49,090 times fewer values, and can by construction compare clients whose architectures differ, at the cost of one pattern optimisation per client per round.


### [38] [Predictive Closure: A Set-Level Characterization of JEPA's Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27364)
*Mosh Levy, Aly Lidayan, Yoav Goldberg*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPA), an encoder and a predictor are trained together to produce a representation that minimizes the error in predicting the representation of the next observation. Because the representations of both the current and the next observation are learned, it is unclear which features they will include. Earlier characterizations describe the features JEPA selects in terms of their individual predictability, raising the concern that unpredictable features, like a traffic light, will not be encoded into the representation. However, the prediction error is also affected by the relationships between features, e.g., the light's color helps predict whether the traffic will stop. We propose the predictive closure characterization, which accounts for these relationships. Based on a reformulation of the JEPA training objective, we show that selection depends on properties of feature sets. This view agrees with earlier works when features evolve independently, but makes new predictions when they do not, for instance that a feature like the traffic light can still be retained. We test this characterization in controlled experiments, which show that unpredictable features can become better represented when they help predict other features, and that changing one feature's predictive relationships can affect how well others are represented. We also demonstrate implications for steering feature selection: targeting one feature with an auxiliary objective, or supplying it to the predictor as side information, can strengthen the representation of another feature whose future values it helps predict, so steering can be indirect.


### [39] [Trail-Weighted Influence Scores for Approximate Outcome Prediction in Bayesian Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27366)
*Sahil Kumar Rajput, Randhir Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study trail-weighted pruning of evidence for Bayesian-network prediction. A traversal enumerates the active trails from each evidence node to the outcome, scores each trail by the product of its edge strengths, and drops evidence whose score falls below a threshold. For collider-free networks (chains and forks) over binary variables, we show that the trail score equals the exact correlation between a trail's endpoints, that a chain and a fork with equal edge strengths have identical joint distributions, and that the pruned posterior is within a computable sum of per-trail terms of the exact posterior when each dropped trail lies in its own branch of the outcome node. We prove this bound, check it exhaustively on all small trees, and show that it fails when dropped trails share a branch. In simulations on random trees, thresholding trail scores gives 1.4 to 13 times lower error than random pruning of the same size. We also apply the score, as an unproven heuristic, to seven bnlearn networks with colliders and loops. Keeping the top half of the active evidence by trail score gives lower error than random pruning on all seven, but it is not clearly better than a mutual-information ranking, and scoring costs more than the inference it saves on six of the seven, so we show no speedup. Models trained on trail, graph and information features and tested on networks they never saw do not beat the trail score or mutual information, but trail features carry most of what they use and clearly outperform generic graph features. A bound for general networks is left to future work.


### [40] [Shaped SAEs: Expanding the Toolkit for Mechanistic Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27375)
*Oveys Delafrooz Noroozi, Bhagyashree Puranik, Upamanyu Madhow*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) seek to decompose the dense activations of large language models (LLMs) into sparse, potentially interpretable features using a learned overcomplete dictionary. In this work, we ask whether it is possible to enhance the semantic selectivity of the learned dictionary atoms by linearly \emph{shaping} the dense activations prior to learning an SAE. We explore rank-reducing transformations based on the recently introduced J-space corresponding to the averaged Jacobian from the current layer to the LLM output, projecting activations onto the ``live'' subspace spanned by eigenmodes of the Jacobian with larger singular values (which we expect to be more semantically relevant). We also introduce a new approach for training SAEs in which (a)~an affine shaping transformation is learned along with the dictionary atoms, (b)~the atom selection mechanism is based on the TopK projections along the one-dimensional subspace spanned by the atoms, and (c)~reconstruction is carried out using the same atoms. We call this approach a Matched Filter SAE (MF-SAE) based on analogies with communication theory. The promise of shaping is demonstrated via evaluations against dense activations and state-of-the-art SAEs on benchmarks including SAEBench and SAE Probes.


### [41] [Sample-efficient learning with Dense Associative Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27384)
*Andrew Lucas, Dmitry Krotov*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We analytically study the evolution of the energy function of Dense Associative Memories (DenseAMs) as they train on randomly sampled codewords of random expander low-density parity-check codes (a.k.a. solutions of random XORSAT problems). We identify two distinct regimes and a transition between them, reminiscent of grokking in neural networks. A regime of small training set size is where the model memorizes a few training examples but cannot discover the underlying rules of the entire codeword dictionary generative process. The opposite regime, corresponding to a larger training set size, is where the model has uncovered the sparse constraints that generated the entire data set after seeing only polynomially many patterns. We also design a novel class of DenseAMs with a thresholding learning rule, and show that any amount of thresholding enables the model to perform simplified sparse Hamiltonian learning, which exponentially reduces the number of training examples needed to learn the true underlying data set. In this regime, the model learns how to discover $\exp[\mathrm{O}(n)]$ solutions from only $\mathrm{O}(\log n)$ training samples, which is asymptotically optimal for any learning algorithm. When training on codewords of certain random codes, the model can even learn the underlying code when a small O(1) fraction of bits are latent variables. These latent variables are not shown to the model during learning, but will be discovered by sparsifying observed higher-order correlations. For certain random codes, we show that a simple and conservative algorithm learns a minimal latent representation for the data set. This provides a transparent, physics-motivated, and analytically tractable toy model for how deep latent representations emerge in an associative memory training.


### [42] [Fixed-Size Active Statistical Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27389)
*Erik Skalnes, Michael Oberst*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Variance reduction techniques improve the efficiency of drawing conclusions from data. Recent proposals for variance-reduction use two complementary techniques to reduce variance: incorporating machine learning predictions into estimators and weighted sampling. A popular weighted sampling algorithm is Poisson Sampling (PS), which independently flips a biased coin at each unit and includes all heads in the sample. A fundamental flaw with PS is that it has a random sample size, which presents practical challenges and introduces variance into the estimator. We show that a fixed-size alternative, known in survey sampling as Conditional Poisson Sampling (CPS), is a drop-in replacement for PS on general multivariate Z-estimation problems, which include convex M-estimation considered in prior work. Notably, we show that the asymptotic variance of CPS is no worse than that of PS, and can be substantially better in realistic scenarios where predictions are imperfect. We illustrate these advantages on both synthetic and real datasets, and release a Python package implementing CPS. In light of our contributions, there is no need to suffer a variable sample size.


### [43] [MLIPS: Multi-Layer Indexer Pool Sharing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27438)
*Samkit Jain, Sai Kishan Pampana, Shlok Agarwal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Sparse attention limits the keys used by each query, but finding those keys can still require a full-prefix scan at every layer. We introduce \textbf{MLIPS (Multi-Layer Indexer Pool Sharing)}, a training-free method that shares candidate search while retaining each consumer's own scoring and selection. A producer publishes a top-$M$ pool larger than the attention budget $K$; subsequent layers select their own top-$K$ from that pool. Measurements across three sparse-attention model families show substantially higher coverage from broad pools than overlap between final selections. We implement layerwise sharing, persistent decode reuse, and a coarse producer in vLLM for DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Across four long-context suites, three consumer layers per producer give $1.14$--$1.40\times$ speedups and $14.8$--$27.4\%$ lower GPU energy, with observed accuracy changes of $-1.49$ to $+0.29$ percentage points in single runs. More extensive sharing exposes task-dependent quality losses. Sampled prefill traces also reveal recurring selected KV positions across query groups and layers. We characterize this shared working-set structure and use it to motivate Top-$G$, an exploratory bounded pool construction for prefill. Its deployment, task accuracy, and serving benefits remain open.


### [44] [Scaling Graph Reasoning in Language Models with Learnable Graph Positional Encodings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27439)
*Javier Porras-Valenzuela, Arush Arora, Alejandro Ribeiro*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Solving reasoning tasks supported on graphs typically requires providing a Language Reasoning Model (LRM) with a serialized description of the graph. This approach results in the input length growing with the number of edges, which limits the size of the problems that can be solved. Furthermore, even when graphs fit in context, performance sharply decays as the graphs grow. We propose to address this by augmenting LRMs with scalable and permutation equivariant graph positional encodings that modulate attention logits. An additional post-fusion-style module incorporates structural information into hidden states. We compute these positional encodings using a GNN followed by a Graph Transformer. This eliminates the need for serialized adjacencies, reducing the context burden to $O(N)$. We jointly fine-tune the positional encoder and the LRM LoRA adapters using optimal solutions generated by graph-theoretic solvers, resulting in a computationally efficient method. Trained on small graphs, the model exhibits improved size-generalization across graph-theoretic tasks on graphs with up to 1,000 nodes, and produces fewer tokens than a conventional LRM. Additionally, we observe that our model remains robust to node relabelings. Evaluations on graph-theoretic benchmarks also show that performance on small graphs is preserved.


### [45] [Using Set Learning to Improve Set-Valued Classifiers on Long-tailed Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27443)
*Kevin Kasa, Scott C. Lowe, Graham W. Taylor*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Set-valued classification (SVC) offers a practical approach to communicating uncertainty while maintaining accuracy by predicting variable-sized sets per input rather than fixed-sized sets (e.g. top-1 or top-5). However, set-valued classifiers (SVCs) exhibit significantly degraded performance on long-tailed and imbalanced datasets, a setting for which they are ostensibly well-suited. This becomes apparent under macro-average evaluation, which weighs all classes equally. SVCs are typically formed during a post-hoc calibration stage, so little is known about how training dynamics affect their behaviour. In this work, we investigate how different neural network training strategies and loss functions impact downstream SVC performance on highly imbalanced data. In addition to methods such as label smoothing and class-balancing, we investigate whether loss functions that operate over sets of examples in a batch, rather than treating all examples as independent, lead to better downstream SVCs. We adapt the recently proposed odd-K-out (OKO) loss function, which learns from sets of training samples, improving its sample efficiency whilst introducing algorithmic modifications that reduce its computational overhead from 3.2x to just 1.36x that of standard cross-entropy training. Experiments on highly long-tailed datasets demonstrate our modified OKO approach significantly improves macro-accuracy across multiple SVC methods - including top-k, average-k, and conformal prediction - while maintaining competitive micro-accuracy. This improved performance on minority classes occurs without substantially increasing prediction set sizes, suggesting that carefully constructed set-based training can produce networks better suited to imbalanced data.


### [46] [Decision Sufficiency of Learned Representation Interfaces for Safe Generative Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27446)
*Jipeng Li, Yanning Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A controller can guide a language model toward safe and useful responses by choosing when to assist, ask for clarification, or refuse. When the controller uses selected internal features, poor performance alone cannot distinguish missing information from limitations of its decision rules or training. We study decision sufficiency: whether the best controller using a representation can match the minimum average loss attainable with full state information, under the same strategies and evaluation criteria. For interfaces with finitely many outputs, we compute the optimal loss exactly and separate the three sources of excess loss. Pairwise checks can miss information loss: states with the same controller input may share an optimal strategy in every pair but not across the whole group. We bound the number of states needed to reveal a conflict and characterize the fewest outputs an unrestricted interface needs to avoid all conflicts. We audit selected features from sparse autoencoders, which encode model activations using relatively few active features, in Qwen2.5, SmolLM2, and Phi. On SmolLM2 responses scored by two human raters, restricting the controller to two selected on/off features increases its minimum average loss by 0.0345--0.1166 relative to full state information, even with the governing policy supplied. Both independently trained autoencoders show the increase on both evaluation sets. The audit identifies decision failures that require changing the controller's input, not merely its architecture or training.


### [47] [Projected Distillation: On Learning to Solve Hard Problems from Close, Correct Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27467)
*Shripad V. Deshmukh, Christian Bock, Kangrui Ruan, Abhijeet Awasthi, Vijay Lingam, Rein Houthooft, Manish Prajapat*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: On-policy learning is central to post-training: training on a model's own generations limits distribution shift and helps preserve existing capabilities. On hard problems, however, on-policy RL provides no reward-based learning signal when all attempts fail. On-policy distillation provides next-token supervision from a teacher, but on hard problems, if a rollout goes wrong early, subsequent supervision is conditioned on prefixes that already contain the mistake. Correct behavior can instead come from a gold or teacher solution, but such off-policy targets can differ substantially from the model's generations and, as we show, may erode existing capabilities. We therefore treat on-policyness as a continuum and propose projected distillation, which minimally edits the student's failed attempts into correct targets. The method uses contrastive updates to favor corrections over failed attempts and refreshes these corrections as the student evolves, enabling near-on-policy learning even when all student attempts fail. Theoretically, we prove that the student's learning regret grows with the edit size: it scales linearly with the horizon under minimal correction, as in DAgger, and quadratically under full-answer replacement, as in behavior cloning. Empirically, across two students (Qwen3-8B and OLMo-2-7B) and five domains spanning math, code, science, and tool use, projected distillation gives the highest area under the specialization-retention frontier (best on 8 of 9 cells against GRPO and OPD, and 7 of 9 against SPOT and SODA).
