Our ablations highlight the importance of online corrections, the contrastive loss, and on-policyness: moving targets farther off-policy steadily worsens the specialization-retention trade-off, with retention falling from 0.916 to 0.744 when minimal corrections are replaced by student-agnostic gold solutions.


### [48] [Training-Free Continual Learning for Multimodal Forecasting via Prompt Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27468)
*Laurent Mombaerts, Jonathan Taws, Jacopo Gargano, Dinesh Gaddam, Kevin George, Srijan Tiwari, Shreyas Rajesh*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Adapting a forecasting system to a changing world classically means retraining. We ask whether the task of continually learning regimes for time series forecasting can be represented by a piece of text: a guidance block, evolved by outcome-driven reflection and used in a frozen LLM forecaster's prompt. On a refreshed multimodal benchmark (weekly search indices, daily currencies, daily commodities, each window paired with web-retrieved news), we run GEPA-style prompt evolution under strict contamination control to prevent the data leakage common in timeseries benchmarks: the forecaster's knowledge cutoff predates every evaluated window. A 900-word evolved guidance carried by Llama-3.3-70B beats TimeSeries Foundation Models (TSFMs) on held-out data (GM MASE 0.776 vs. 0.898 for TabPFN-TS-3), transfers to more recent models without measurable loss, and costs dollars rather than GPU-days to update. We characterize the loop: out-of-loop gains concentrate in the first reflection, in-loop improvements overstate out-of-loop gains 2-3x unless an untouched monitor arbitrates, and error-only objectives silently suppress the text modality. We provide two extensions. First, compiling the guidance into executable code with a TSFM as a callable tool recovers most of the gain deterministically. Second, ablations show the advantage widens at short horizons and that removing the news text does not hurt the guided forecaster, consistent with the text's value being distilled into policy during learning. We argue that continual prompt evolution is a practical, training-free alternative to retraining for multimodal forecasting.


### [49] [When Parameter Gradient Flows Close in Representation Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27474)
*Shengye Tao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The represented gradient flow of a parameterized model depends on a mobility that can retain hidden parameter information. We study when one entire represented mobility map works for every autonomous loss from one fixed initialization. For two-factor matrix representations wider than both output dimensions, with individually full-row-rank factors, we classify all admissible joint ranks: closure occurs precisely at effective scalar-balanced minimal joint rank or a two-value signed spectral family at full joint rank. All intermediate ranks are excluded; positive represented flows belong to known square-root families. For general representations with constant ambient representation and gradient-Lie ranks, ordinary closure is equivalent to a local invariant-level metric in the analytic case and specified smooth regimes. An endpoint section forces the initially unrestricted mobility map to admit a locally smooth representative on the represented image. We also separate testing families: linear losses locally admit closure at every submersion, whereas one fixed translated strongly convex quadratic family fails it on a delayed-bracket example. A fixed output readout gives an ordinary squared-loss witness. These supporting results do not characterize quadratic-only closure in general. We retain the unresolved smooth output-rank-two case with Lie rank at least four. A frozen deterministic experiment checks a separate engineered-loss construction; it is distinguished from the squared-loss proof, universal theorems and empirical learning performance.


### [50] [Orthogonal Boundary Unlearning for Dual-Use Concepts in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27490)
*Niccolò Ronchetti*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Mitigating catastrophic risks from large language models requires excising hazardous operational capabilities, such as cyberwarfare exploitation and pathogen enhancement, without degrading benign scientific utility. In these dual-use domains, legitimate defensive science and malicious attacks share vocabulary, syntax, and internal model representations, causing standard unlearning methods to induce severe collateral damage (concept bleeding). In this work, we present Orthogonal Boundary Unlearning (OBU), a mathematically grounded framework designed to eliminate collateral damage on benign dual-use capabilities. OBU extracts a domain-matched capability subspace from paired calibration queries via singular value decomposition on empirical activation covariances, and restricts parameter updates strictly to the orthogonal null space. This guarantees that benign representations experience zero parameter perturbation along the spanned calibration subspace. We present two complementary realizations of OBU: Projected LoRA, which applies the orthogonal constraint during iterative gradient-based fine-tuning, and Closed-Form Weight Surgery, which directly edits model weights in a single analytical step without training. Across four modern open-weight foundation models (Gemma-3-4B, Qwen3.5-4B, SmolLM3-3B, and Granite-4.2-3B), OBU achieves strong dual-use concept separation, substantially mitigates in-context priming attacks, and demonstrates durable resistance against adversarial relearning without degrading benign domain competence.


### [51] [Self-Evolving Agents through Privileged Retrospective Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27495)
*Yuhang Zhou, Yifan Wu, Bo Peng, Mingyi Wang, Jiayi Liu, Xiangjun Fan, Lizhu Zhang, Zhuokai Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Self-evolving agents aim to improve from their own experience rather than remain fixed after deployment, but naturally collected trajectories often lack ground-truth solutions or verifier-derived rewards. We introduce Retrospective Self-Distillation (RSD), a multi-round framework that turns such experience into dense supervision using privileged retrospective context. For each on-policy trajectory, the same underlying model acts as both student and teacher: the teacher re-scores the student's realized actions while conditioning on cross-task precedents and lessons, together with turn-level reflection and future hindsight from the completed trajectory. The resulting teacher distribution is distilled back into the policy through on-policy self-distillation. Across rounds, the experience bank grows and the retrospective context is refreshed, allowing the model, its data, and its supervision to co-evolve. We further introduce frontier-guided context-policy evolution, where a stronger frontier model proposes constrained edits to how privileged context is constructed. Across ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, and SearchQA with Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B, RSD improves performance by up to 59.7% relative to the base model, while frontier-guided context-policy evolution adds up to a further 44.7% relative improvement over a fixed context policy. These results show that naturally accumulated experience can be converted into persistent model capability without ground-truth trajectories or verifier-derived rewards for the core policy update.


### [52] [BEACON: Bridging Multimodal Semantics and Asynchronous Propagation for Cloud System Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27542)
*Zhe Li, Tengxue Zhang, Yang Shu, Tung Kieu, Lunting Fan, Yingying Zhang, Bin Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Modern large-scale cloud systems continuously generate massive and multimodal observability data, such as time-series metrics, log records, and system dependency graphs, which reflect the health state of the system. To ensure reliable cloud system operation, anomaly detection models identify abnormal states by analyzing these multimodal observations. However, existing approaches rely on simple fusion strategies and graph learning, ignoring the semantic gap among modalities and the asynchronous spatio-temporal dependencies across system instances over time. To this end, we propose BEACON, a framework to Bridge multimodal sEmantics and Asynchronous propagation for Cloud system anOmaly detectioN. Specifically, we introduce a Dynamic Interpretable Multimodal Aggregator (DIMA) that leverages an information-bottleneck mechanism to compress information from the semantics and temporal dynamics of metrics, and aligns them with logs in a shared prototype space. Furthermore, we propose an Asynchronous Spatio-Temporal Propagator (ASTP) that captures temporal dependencies with multi-scale temporal attention and models spatial relations with topology-aware aggregation. This enables asynchronous propagation of anomalies across system instances. Extensive experiments on three real-world cloud system datasets demonstrate that \textbf{BEACON} consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in anomaly detection.


### [53] [Timing-Aware and Interpretable Self-Attention for Event-Based Wearable Task Transition Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27549)
*Anda Ouyang, Siyuan Chen, Julien Epps*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Task transition detection aims to identify when a person shifts from one task to another, providing a foundation for continuous health monitoring and timely context-aware interventions. While statistical and deep feature representations have demonstrated strong classification performance, they often rely on aggregated or latent features that are difficult to interpret in behavioural terms. Event-based features provide a compact and interpretable alternative by encoding behavioural and physiological patterns as discrete tokens with explicit semantic meaning, making them well suited for behavioural analysis in health informatics. Self-attention offers a mechanism for modelling interactions among such tokens; however, conventional self-attention classifiers primarily rely on token order and do not explicitly incorporate timing or duration information. As a result, informative temporal cues in event representations may not be fully exploited for task transition detection. This study proposes an event-based time-aware self-attention framework that represents behavioural signals as sequences of discrete events augmented with onset timestamps and durations. Event timing is encoded using a parameter-efficient kernel-based representation, while a novel type-aware duration embedding modulates information propagation according to event persistence. Experiments on a semi-naturalistic wearable dataset show that the proposed model outperforms existing event-based approaches, achieves performance beyond or comparable with statistical and deep-learning models with a more compact input representation, and provides improved interpretability through integrated-gradient-based analysis. These results indicate that explicit modelling of event timing and duration enhances both classification performance and interpretability, supporting reliable deployment in biomedical and health informatics applications.


### [54] [Lower Bounds for Smooth Nonconvex Zeroth-Order Optimization: The Price of Determinism under Low Curvature Trace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27550)
*Yuxing Peng, Zhiqing Tang, Weijia Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Low curvature trace, an effective rank condition, is a common explanation for why randomized zeroth-order methods scale to high-dimensional tasks such as language model fine-tuning. Can deterministic methods achieve the same savings? We show that they cannot. We study finding $\varepsilon$-stationary points of $L$-smooth nonconvex functions on $\mathbb{R}^d$ from exact function values, assuming that below the starting value the Hessian is dominated by a fixed matrix with trace at most $LE$; this is the effective rank condition used to explain zeroth-order fine-tuning. When $ET\le d$, the Gaussian two-point method that such fine-tuning uses needs only $O(ET)$ calls if it rejects steps that raise $f$, where $T=L\Delta/\varepsilon^2$ is the iteration count of gradient descent from initial gap $\Delta$, and we prove that every randomized method needs $\Omega(ET)$ calls, so this cost is optimal up to constants. In contrast, every deterministic method needs $\widetilde\Omega(d\min\{T,\sqrt{ET}\})$ calls, and adaptive subspace search attains $O(d\min\{T,\sqrt{ET}\})$: a deterministic method pays about $d$ calls per discovered direction, and the trace budget allows about $\sqrt{ET}$ directions. Determinism thus multiplies the cost by a factor of order $d/(E+\sqrt{ET})$ up to logarithmic factors, which grows linearly with $d$ for fixed $E$ and $T$; once $E$ exceeds $T$ by a logarithmic factor, deterministic methods need $\Omega(dT)$ calls, as without any trace budget. Our lower bounds hold for $C^\infty$ functions with the matrix bound on all of $\mathbb{R}^d$, even when each call also returns gradient projections priced by rank.


### [55] [Can Native Safety Travel? Reusing Safety Pretraining Across Models via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27555)
*Bingqi Shang, Yang Zhang, Yiwei Chen, Changsheng Wang, Shiyu Chang, Bingquan Shen, Sijia Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Safety pretraining has emerged as a promising paradigm for embedding safety deeply into a model during pretraining, yielding robustness to subsequent fine-tuning that conventional safety post-training often lacks. We refer to this safety acquired during model pretraining as native safety. Yet native safety is expensive to acquire, confined to the model in which it was pretrained, and often accompanied by lower general utility. In this work, we rethink the practical value of safety-pretrained models and introduce, for the first time, the native safety reuse problem: Can native safety be transferred from a specialized, potentially smaller, safety-pretrained "teacher" to standard-pretrained "students," enabling them to inherit its robustness while preserving their general utility? We show that native safety can "travel" through on-policy distillation. A standard-pretrained student generates responses to safety-pretraining prompts, while a small safety-pretrained teacher supervises the student's token-level predictions along its own generated trajectories; cross-tokenizer alignment further enables transfer across model families. The resulting students exhibit substantially greater safety robustness than their safety-post-trained counterparts while retaining higher general utility than the teacher: subsequent fine-tuning raises the average attack success rate (ASR) of the 1.7B student by only 5.00%, compared with 11.58% for safety post-training, and its average utility of 48.20% far exceeds the aligned teacher's 27.42%. Unlike conventional large-to-small knowledge distillation, our framework enables selective, small-to-large safety transfer: it reuses the robust safety expertise of a smaller pretrained teacher without inheriting its utility limitations. These findings establish native safety as a reusable model property rather than one permanently tied to the originally pretrained model.


### [56] [AnchorRM: Parallel Intra-Batch State Reconstruction with Anchor-Based Residual Memory for Temporal Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27558)
*Haroon Gharwi, Kai Shu, Yue Dai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Memory-based temporal graph neural networks (TGN) rely on recurrent node memories to capture evolving interaction histories. However, temporal mini-batching introduces a trade-off between computational efficiency and temporal fidelity: when a node appears multiple times within the same batch, later interactions are evaluated using stale pre-batch memory because the intermediate memory updates have not yet been committed. This problem becomes more severe as temporal batch size increases. To overcome this challenge, we propose AnchorRM, an anchor-based residual memory mechanism that reconstructs event-specific intra-batch node states without executing sequential recurrent updates. AnchorRM treats the last committed node memory at the beginning of each batch as a pre-batch fixed anchor and uses causally preceding same-batch interactions to infer the missing state evolution relative to that anchor. The learned residual then calibrates the anchor to reconstruct an event-specific node memory for each interaction. Because each event-specific residual is predicted directly from the fixed anchor and its causal history, reconstructed states do not depend on previously reconstructed states and can therefore be computed in parallel within the batch while preserving temporal causality. Experiments across four temporal graph datasets and three memory-based backbones demonstrate that AnchorRM achieves an average 6.16x training speedup (up to 10.05x) and 28.67x batch scaling (up to 40x). These results demonstrate that AnchorRM substantially improves the scalability and training efficiency of memory-based temporal graph learning while maintaining competitive predictive performance.


### [57] [TRCM: Topology-Aware Route-Consistency Modeling for Robust Neural Vehicle Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27568)
*Peiheng Jian, Zhenwei Wang, Tiehua Zhang, Lei Zhang, Zhishu Shen, Ruibin Bai, Zhijun Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization has enabled fast constructive solvers for the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP), yet most autoregressive models still construct solutions through sequential node selection and represent route organization only implicitly. We investigate whether route-level customer co-assignment can be modeled explicitly and used to guide this node-level decision process. To this end, we propose TopologyAware RouteConsistency Modeling (TRCM), which reuses node representations from a standard autoregressive construction pipeline to predict pairwise same-route probabilities and injects them into customer selection as a route-consistency signal. Because sameroute supervision is both class-imbalanced and structurally uneven, TRCM combines structure-preserving augmentation with Topological Entropy (TE)- based reweighting to emphasize locally ambiguous customer pairs. Under matched decoding budgets, TRCM reduces the optimality gap from 3.67% to 3.28% on CVRP50 and from 2.11% to 1.99% on CVRP100; under 8-fold augmented inference, it reduces the CVRP100 gap from 1.61% to 1.49%. On XML100, TRCM*8Aug improves the average objective by 0.24%, with larger gains on several structurally informative factors. A controlled demand-reassignment test over 500 variants further shows more concentrated objective fluctuations. These results support explicit route-consistency modeling as an effective structural principle for autoregressive neural routing.


### [58] [Two Is Enough: Binary Classification Priors Suffice for Multi-Class In-Context Tabular Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27569)
*Zhixiong Cheng, Shiyuan Han, Jingjing Suo, Zhengli Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) pretrain a transformer on large-scale synthetic priors so that a single forward pass yields zero-shot tabular prediction. TabICLv2 samples the class count of its classification prior during pretraining, and the prevailing configuration caps it at ten. We report a counter-intuitive result: setting this cap directly to two---making every pretrained classification task binary---still yields weights that perform zero-shot K-way classification 2. On the 38 TabArena classification datasets (200 bootstrap rounds, anchored at default RF = 1000), the binary two-stage recipe reaches 1590.1 Elo, above the official default at 1568.0 (+22.1), above TabPFN-v2.6 (1577.1) and RealTabPFN-v2.5 (1521.6). A production-scale validation on 60 real-world credit-scoring institutions independently confirms the direction: the same checkpoint improves test AUC on 44 of 60 institutions (paired Wilcoxon p = 0.0007 for AUC, p = 0.0002 for KS) at +1.5 inference cost. Mechanistically, classification in TabICLv2 is carried not by a fixed-cardinality output head but by an in-context comparison operator matching query-row embeddings against class-conditional embeddings, so the cardinality of the output space is set by the labels present in the context, not by the label space seen during pretraining. This makes a binary prior structurally cheaper to generate and cache than a ten-class prior, demotes "number of classes" from an architectural constraint to a tunable hyperparameter, and points to a class-count-agnostic regime of TFM pretraining.


### [59] [From POMDP Theory to Deep RL with Particle Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27575)
*Kevin Tan, Ziping Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning in partially observable environments requires inferring hidden states, learning latent dynamics, and planning under the resulting belief. Practical algorithms often solve one aspect of the above pipeline while relying on approximations for the others that are often poorly understood, while existing POMDP theory typically analyzes computationally inefficient theoretical algorithms far from modern deep RL practice. To fill this gap, we propose a practical end-to-end algorithm that approximately solves the hidden state filtering problem via sequential Monte Carlo (SMC), updates a latent dynamics model online, and performs approximate online planning for $d$ steps before bootstrapping leaves with a state-value critic (a QMDP approximation). We analyze each approximation and prove an end-to-end regret bound decomposing the error into model estimation error, particle filter error, Monte Carlo rollout error, and a structural bias term induced by the QMDP approximation controllable by the planning depth that we further analyze in various special cases. The resulting guarantee makes explicit how statistical sample size, particle budget, rollout budget, and planning depth affect performance. We instantiate our algorithm in an MPC-based deep RL framework, demonstrating its practicality on a variety of environments. On a POMDP that satisfies our assumptions, each term of the bound decays at its predicted rate. Our results provide a practical and theoretically grounded route for bridging theory and practice within online decision-making in POMDPs.


### [60] [Bridging Digital Deception and Physical Fidelity: A Spatio-Temporal Attack on Cognitive Radars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27585)
*Tongrun Xing, Yongjun Dai, Xuan Liao, Zhiwen Zhang, Tong Zhou, Longlong Zhang, Tian Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: As cognitive radars evolve towards the end-to-end processing of one-dimensional complex baseband sequences, deep networks face severe adversarial threats. However, existing attacks encounter dual limitations in practical electronic warfare: low cross-architecture transferability and severe vulnerability to physical channel distortions, which degrades their tactical reliability. To overcome these bottlenecks, we propose a physical prior-driven Segmented Spatio-Temporal Attack (SSTA). First, a global-local feature decoupling segmentation mechanism interrupts the adversarial gradients' dependence on long-range sequence coherence, bridging the cross-architecture feature gap. Second, moving beyond static norm constraints devoid of physical semantics, we abstract channel distortions into differentiable spatio-temporal operators, integrating them into momentum iterations via an Expectation over Transformation (EoT) strategy. Comprehensive cross-evaluations across 9 heterogeneous models spanning CNNs, RNNs, and Transformers demonstrate that SSTA outperforms the state-of-the-art baseline by 10.36% in Average Black-Box Attack Success Rate. Furthermore, baseband equivalent simulations confirm that the generated waveforms survive extreme physical distortions and, after matched filtering, achieve zero main-lobe attenuation (0 dB) with noise floor lift strictly within Constant False Alarm Rate tolerances. SSTA successfully unifies digital-domain deception and physical-domain fidelity, providing crucial support for CEW security assessment.


### [61] [Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion: The Evaluation Evidence Behind Recursive Self-Improvement Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27610)
*Zihan Chen, Di Zhu, Lei Nico Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) promises AI systems, such as self-evolving LLM agents, that become better at producing further improvements. Evaluating this promise requires separating product claims about the final system from process claims about the improvement process itself. Existing surveys specify the evidence these claims require, but how often studies report that evidence remains unmeasured. We examine this question through full-text coding of 114 eligible studies in a reproducible arXiv search frame frozen in October 2026, including 90 that describe their own systems as RSI. The codebook links claims to evaluation designs. Independent human coding, third-reader adjudication, and a separate blind coding of improver comparisons assess coding reliability. Among the 90 RSI studies, 90% report product evaluation beyond development tasks under a broad criterion that includes partial or unstated separation, while 60% report evaluation on tasks unused for selection. Only 22% audit two or more reruns of the full loop. In a human-coded random sample, the RSI studies show the same contrast: 45% evaluate on tasks unused for selection and 14% audit two or more reruns. Of the 26 studies claiming a better improvement procedure, 23 report a related comparison, but only 4 compare earlier and later procedures from identical starting artifacts at a matched budget. Across these results and a comparison set of established works, evidence for repeatability and improved procedures remains less developed than product evaluation. Three propositions explain how selection effects, shared evaluator errors, and changes in system state limit what product evaluations can establish. Together, the findings support a claim-indexed reporting standard that connects each claim to the evaluation design needed to test it.


### [62] [Learning Inter-Graph Interactions Between Heterogeneous Substructures of Chemical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27614)
*Gyoung S. Na*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Complex chemical systems containing heterogeneous substructures are common in real-world applications. Various physical phenomena of the complex chemical systems are derived from the interactions between the heterogeneous substructures. However, existing graph representation learning methods for inter-graph interactions assumed graph-level interactions between homogeneous structures, such as organic molecules and inorganic crystalline materials. We propose a data descriptor of the complex chemical systems and a graph neural network for learning inter-graph interactions between organic and inorganic compounds. We applied the proposed method to predict the physical properties of hybrid solar cell materials containing heterogeneous substructures, which have received significant attention for sustainable energy resources. By learning heterogeneous inter-graph interactions, the proposed method achieved state-of-the-art accuracy in predicting band gaps of 1,682 hybrid solar cell materials.


### [63] [From Outcomes to Steps: Reliable Success-Aware Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27623)
*Jiaxi Liu, Shixiong Kai, Zhentao Tang, Yang Li, Yuqi Cui, Mingxuan Yuan, Junchi Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for long-horizon language agents requires identifying whether intermediate actions make eventual task success more or less likely when supervision is available only at trajectory level. The main difficulty is that a critic with aggregate return-prediction accuracy may still fail to resolve the local value differences needed to assign credit to actions, especially under sparse feedback and function approximation. Conventional critics estimate future return, but policy improvement also depends on knowing whether each intermediate decision makes task success more or less likely. We introduce SCALE, an actor-critic framework that augments conventional return estimation with explicit prediction of eventual task success. At each interaction prefix, the critic predicts a return value and a Success Value, defined as the probability that the task will eventually be completed from that prefix. SCALE converts changes in Success Value into multi-step action credit through Success-GAE and modulates its contribution according to the estimated reliability of the predictor, while retaining the token-level PPO objective. Across five agent benchmarks and three model backbones, SCALE improves task performance over the compared RL baselines in most settings, with gains of up to 4.1 percentage points in success rate. Separate critic-learning analyses show that SCALE also improves critic estimation and training stability.


### [64] [Ensemble-Aware Structural Encoding (EASE): Conditioning Protein Encoders on Peptide Conformational Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27638)
*Ruoyao Zhang, Hongxuan Li, Jie Peng, Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Protein foundation models provide transferable representations for downstream peptide prediction tasks. However, existing representations typically reduce a peptide to a single sequence or structure, which may miss conformational variability relevant to key peptide properties, including antibacterial activity, hemolysis, toxicity, stability, and binding. For example, protease resistance correlates more strongly with conformational dynamics measured by hydrogen exchange than with average helicity. Therefore, we introduce Ensemble-Aware Structural Encoding (EASE), a framework for effectively extracting structural information from conformational ensembles for peptide representation. To extract useful information from noisy conformers, EASE selects central conformers representing a peptide's major structural states and fuses their individual representations with a permutation-invariant operator to produce a peptide-level representation for downstream prediction. We then evaluate our framework on six peptide property-prediction tasks under leakage-controlled splits. Against matched single-structure baselines, EASE improves performance consistently on six tasks with a mean absolute gain of about 3.0% across two structural encoders. Gains keep growing as we add conformers through K=20. A single peptide conformer therefore provides a limited basis for structural representation, whereas our framework distilling and fusing multiple conformers yields a more informative representation.


### [65] [BCT: Bayes-Consistent Tempering for Flow Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27641)
*Daisuke Oba, Hiroki Furuta, Naoaki Okazaki*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Temperature scaling is typically applied as a post-hoc transformation of model probabilities. In posterior-induced flow language models, this creates two mismatches. First, posterior tempering implicitly changes both the endpoint distribution and the distribution of intermediate noisy states, while the denoiser remains trained on the original states. Second, the tempered posterior weights are combined with transport dynamics derived for the untempered flow. These mismatches are reflected empirically: lowering temperature improves GSM8K performance much less reliably in flow LMs than in discrete diffusion LMs. We introduce Bayes-Consistent Tempering (BCT), which makes temperature explicit in both the distributions and dynamics of the generative flow. Its learned realization trains the denoiser on tempered intermediate states and tempers the endpoint distribution using the corresponding tempered dynamics. We also derive training-free variants for conventionally trained checkpoints. Across hyperspherical flow LMs with two denoiser architectures and a von Mises--Fisher flow LM, BCT improves GSM8K generation across broad temperature ranges and achieves the strongest tempering performance. The gains also extend to Sudoku, demonstrating benefits on tightly constrained generation. At very low temperatures, the learned realization is also markedly more robust than the training-free variants, supporting temperature as a property of the generative flow across training and inference rather than a post-hoc probability adjustment. Code and model weights are available at this https URL .


### [66] [Pairwise Learning Increases Data Efficiency in Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27669)
*Robert Bain, Marjan Rashidi, Elizabeth Chrastil*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep neural networks often fail at small-sample regression, collapsing to low-variance predictions that miss individual differences. Here, we introduce a pairwise comparison framework that reframes regression as aggregated relative rankings. This method improves data efficiency across model architectures and dataset sizes. On the UTKFace dataset (23k images), pairwise learning generates over one billion training comparisons without additional supervision. The paired ranking allows a lightweight ResNet10 trained from scratch on home hardware to achieve a 3.64-year MAE-surpassing previous models, including those with large-scale pretraining. For classification into categories, pairwise supervision consistently outperforms an otherwise identical 10-way crossentropy model on STL10 with 500-2000 labeled examples, improving accuracy by up to 5.2%. Overall, pairwise supervision enhances data efficiency and enables strong regression and classification performance when labeled data are limited, particularly in clinical settings.


### [67] [Neural Operator Splitting for Fluid Dynamics in Complex Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27671)
*Jianxiang Li, Xi Deng, Hong Chul Nam, Anima Anandkumar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Solving dynamical systems in complex and diverse geometries remains challenging for both numerical solvers and learning-based surrogate models. We propose Neural Operator Splitting, a framework for learning fluid dynamics while preserving the physical structure of fractional-step numerical schemes. Instead of learning the full-step evolution monolithically, we decompose each time step into physically interpretable steps, and learn each operator separately using intermediate states from the underlying numerical solver. Guided by the linearity properties of the fractional steps, we introduce a conditional-linear Fourier neural operator (CL-FNO) whose linear state mappings are modulated by nonlinear conditioning on inputs such as boundary geometry. We further introduce two complementary strategies, symmetry sampling and corrective cycling, to mitigate error accumulation during autoregressive rollout. Experiments on 2D and 3D fluid flows with varying geometries and initial conditions show that our full model reduces mean relative $L^2$ rollout error by 68\% in 2D and 40\% in 3D compared with a single full-step neural operator. At comparable error levels, neural-operator inference is approximately $10^3$ times faster per step than the Monte Carlo solver.


### [68] [Hybrid Momentum Methods for Optimizing Deep Learning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27674)
*Haifeng Xu, Yijin Ren, Qi Deng, Chenyu Zhou, Dongdong Ge, Yinyu Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Momentum is central to stochastic optimization in deep learning, including the training of large language models. Classical momentum methods typically rely on a fast exponential moving average (EMA) scheme, which discounts past gradients on a fixed timescale and may underweight older gradients that remain informative. This creates a trade-off between retaining long-term history and adapting to recent changes. We introduce Hybrid Momentum (HyM), which combines a linearly weighted average of past gradients with a geometrically discounted fast momentum. The former assigns weights proportional to the gradients' iteration indices and retains a growing span of history, while the latter emphasizes recent gradients. This hybrid construction is modular and can be combined with adaptive preconditioning and other update transformations. In particular, we propose HyMUON, which applies Newton--Schulz orthogonalization to the hybrid direction. Illustrative examples and controlled experiments demonstrate the benefits of combining gradient information across these two timescales. Across image classification, machine translation, and language modeling, HyM and HyMUON match or improve upon strong baselines. HyMUON improves over Muon on IWSLT14, and in both the 100k- and 600k-step GPT-2 124M OpenWebText regimes. For LLaMA-1.3B pretraining on C4, HyMUON achieves a final validation loss of 2.777, compared with 2.903 for Muon.


### [69] [Influence Is the Target-Response Jacobian Times Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27689)
*Paul Geertsema, Simon Geertsema*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce the target-response Jacobian $J = [\partial \hat y_i/\partial y_j]$, which measures how model predictions respond to changes in individual training targets. $J$ complements training-data attribution (TDA) methods, which characterize sensitivity to the weights placed on training observations in the loss rather than to their targets, and can be computed matrix-free at the same cost as influence-based TDA methods. We show that sample-weight influence $\mathcal{I}$ and $J$ follow from the same implicit differentiation argument and differ only by a per-example multiplier: for pointwise losses, $\mathcal{I} = J\operatorname{diag}(m)$ exactly at any stationary point with an invertible Hessian. For canonical-link losses (squared error, logistic, Poisson) the multiplier is the training residual, $\mathcal{I} = J\operatorname{diag}(r)$, and softmax cross-entropy obeys a multiclass version. The identity predicts three ways in which influence fails when used in place of $J$: small residuals shrink its predicted shift towards zero (interpolation collapse), residuals of mixed sign wash out its direction (direction washout), and under softmax cross-entropy it collapses a training point's per-class sensitivities towards its own true class (true-class collapse). We score both operators against refitting with a per-prediction $R^2$ across target perturbations, with no rescaling. On tanh networks trained with squared error (Friedman #1) and logistic loss (MAGIC, HIGGS), with $N$ from 2,000 to $10^6$, $J$ reaches $R^2$ between 0.99 and 1.00, whereas influence's $R^2$ is negative on every dataset. On an MNIST softmax classifier, influence's $R^2$ is again negative, while $J$ reaches 0.92. On HIGGS, one row of $J$, holding all $10^6$ sensitivities of one prediction, takes under 0.2 seconds on an RTX 3090 after a one-time setup of under five minutes, whereas refitting would take $10^6$ refits.


### [70] [Understanding Data Augmentation under Label Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27701)
*Hong Zheng, Fei Teng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Data augmentation (DA) has become a fundamental component of modern machine learning systems and is widely regarded as an effective strategy for improving robustness and generalization. Despite its empirical success, the theoretical impact of DA under label noise remains poorly understood. In this work, we first theoretically explain when DA is beneficial and when it becomes detrimental, and provide sufficient conditions for distinguishing between these two regimes under both the i.i.d. learning setting and the more challenging domain generalization scenario. Then, we reveal a counterintuitive phenomenon: DA essentially acts as an uncertainty amplifier in the learning process, decreasing model stability and generalization performance. This finding is supported by the proposed learning and generalization bounds. Our analyses do not depend on specific DA techniques and require only mild assumptions, indicating that the results are applicable to a broad range of machine learning scenarios. Namely, we provide a unified theoretical foundation for understanding, analyzing, and designing DA methods in the presence of label noise.


### [71] [Multi-Objective Evolutionary Semi-Supervised Learning for Resource-Aware Smart City Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27703)
*Francisco José Sedeño, Jamal Toutouh*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Smart-city applications often combine scarce labeled data with abundant unlabeled observations. Semi-supervised learning (SSL) exploits both labeled and unlabeled data to improve classification under limited supervision. This article presents MOEA-SSL, a multi-objective evolutionary approach for lightweight semi-supervised tabular classification that jointly optimizes predictive quality and feature usage. Experimental evaluation on three Smart City classification datasets, considering 1\%, 5\%, and 10\% labeled data, shows that MOEA-SSL achieves competitive predictive performance compared with established lightweight SSL methods while significantly reducing feature usage relative to EA-SSL in all evaluated conditions. The computed Pareto fronts also provide lower-feature-use alternatives with limited degradation in classification performance.


### [72] [Privacy Accounting for Empirical Denoisers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27710)
*William Cheng, Mijung Park, Dongjun Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion models have emerged as the foundation of modern generative systems, yet their high memorization capacity raises privacy concerns. While differentially private (DP) training provides formal guarantees, it comes at a cost in sample quality. In this work, we study a complementary question: how much privacy loss is incurred during the sampling process itself? Directly analyzing modern neural denoisers is challenging because their sensitivity to a single training example is generally intractable. To overcome this challenge, we construct and analyze a clipped privacy-accountable empirical denoiser, a training-free estimator of the Bayes-optimal denoiser, whose per-step sensitivity can be computed explicitly, allowing each denoising step to be a Gaussian mechanism. This perspective enables privacy accounting under Gaussian DP when each training example contributes to each denoising step, and under $f$-DP trade-off functions when each generated sample uses a single randomly subsampled block of the training data. We report every $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ guarantee as a certified upper bound from a privacy loss distribution accountant, and use a cumulant-based Edgeworth approximation as a fast diagnostic, characterizing where it is reliable and where it becomes anti-conservative. We also analyze the privacy loss along the denoising trajectory and show that it grows steeply near the critical window in which semantic content is formed. This observation motivates hybrid samplers that use the private empirical denoiser through the formative steps where it remains a faithful proxy for the neural denoiser, then switch to a public neural denoiser for the remaining refinement. Our results on CIFAR-10 and CelebA-64 show that the privacy of diffusion models depends not only on the training procedure, but also on sampling-time design choices such as subsampling, stopping time, and hybrid denoising.


### [73] [EEG-Based Classification of Hypnotic Susceptibility Using Two-Dimensional Convolutional Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27738)
*Krzysztof Wrobel, Grzegorz M. Wojcik, Filip Postepski, Andrzej Kawiak, Katarzyna Zemla, Grzegorz Sedek*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Guided Imagery (GI) is a mind-based relaxation technique widely used in clinical and therapeutic settings. Objectively assessing whether a patient has entered a relaxed state remains a challenge for clinicians. This study investigates electroencephalography (EEG)-based classification of hypnotic susceptibility using two-dimensional Convolutional Neural Networks (2D CNNs) applied to minimally preprocessed signals, without manual feature extraction. Data were collected from 62 male participants (32 Control, 30 Hypno) using a 256-channel dense array amplifier during Guided Imagery or cognitive task conditions. A sliding-window framework evaluated up to 868 independent models across seven anatomically defined brain regions and 124 ten-second time windows. Segment-level random-split screening was followed by Leave-One-Subject-Out (LOSO) cross-validation to obtain subject-independent performance estimates, and an ensemble approach combined predictions across K = 10 windows. The best single-window model achieved accuracy of 0.82 on the random split. Under LOSO, anterior regions generalised better across subjects: the frontal region achieved mean accuracy of 0.646 (AUC: 0.692). An ensemble of K = 10 windows further improved the prefrontal region to mean accuracy of 0.738. The occipital region, dominant in random-split screening, collapsed to near-chance under LOSO, revealing subject-specific EEG fingerprinting rather than genuine group differences. Bandpass filtering and z-score normalisation suffice for competitive EEG classification without manual feature extraction. Anterior cortical regions are identified as the most generalisable neural markers of hypnotic susceptibility.


### [74] [Constant Step Sizes for Muon-Type Spectral Optimizers on Strongly Convex Quadratics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27747)
*Pratik Jawanpuria, Bhabatosh Garai, Bamdev Mishra*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Muon and related optimizers update a weight matrix with a spectrally transformed gradient. In this work, we study such spectral optimizers with a constant step size on strongly convex matrix quadratics, which isolate the effect of the update from the confounds of nonconvexity, noise, and step size schedules. We analyze an update using two quantities: the gain, which is the factor by which it amplifies the gradient along each singular direction, and the length, which is the norm of the update. These two quantities place the popular spectral updates in three regimes with different behavior: (i) with a bounded gain, the iterates behave like preconditioned gradient descent and converge for step sizes set by the largest curvature; (ii) with an unbounded gain but a vanishing length, they still converge, with the admissible step sizes set by the total curvature instead; and (iii) with a non-vanishing length, they do not converge for almost every initialization. Interestingly, the practical variants of Muon fall in all three regimes, depending only on how the update is normalized and scaled and not on the polynomial that approximates the polar factor. Experiments validate these findings.


### [75] [Detection of Memorization in Diffusion Models Through Thermodynamic Singularity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27754)
*Iljung Kim, Kangwoo Park, Yung-Kyun Noh, Daniel D. Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In diffusion models, proper distributional learning entails reversing a diffusion process over underlying density functions, whereas a key failure is memorization, in which the reverse process instead learns diffusion from individual training samples. We provide an analytical characterization of such memorized reverse processes, along with a criterion that detects the associated thermodynamic singularity. The proposed criterion reliably distinguishes between properly learned and memorizing networks across a range of neural network models designed to promote or mitigate memorization.


### [76] [AC/DC on a Budget - Alternating Sparse Phases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27758)
*Rahul Nittala, Advait Gadhikar, Tom Jacobs, Rebekka Burkholz*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: State-of-the-art neural network sparsification relies on alternating dense and sparse training phases. While computationally expensive, dense phases are critical for performance. Our empirical analysis reveals the mechanics behind this gain: upon reintroduction, previously masked parameters receive disproportionately large gradients that drive essential mask rewiring and parameter sign-flipping. Building on these insights, we optimize the performance-to-FLOPs Pareto frontier through three innovations: (1) zero-initializing masked parameters during dense phases to promote relevant sign flips, (2) shortening dense phases, and (3) replacing the dense phase entirely with a sparse rewiring phase that updates only the active mask and a small, high-gradient fraction of out-of-mask weights. Evaluated on CIFAR100 and ImageNet, our variant ARC (Alternating Rewiring Compression) matches baseline AC/DC performance at a fraction of the computational cost, establishing a superior performance-to-FLOPs frontier also compared to standard sparse-to-sparse algorithms. Experiments with GPT-2 on OpenWebText further show improvements in validation perplexity and average zero-shot performance


### [77] [Foundation-Style Pretraining for Scalable and Transferable Distributed PV Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27763)
*Dayin Chen, Xiaodan Shi, Xin Xu, Zhongyue Su, Shibo Zhu, Ruizhe Deng, Dongxiao Zhang, Yuntian Chen, Jinyue Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The rapid growth of distributed photovoltaic (PV) generation is increasing the demand for accurate forecasting across large numbers of geographically dispersed stations. Existing approaches are commonly developed for individual sites or specific forecasting horizons, requiring repeated training and maintenance as new stations are integrated and limiting direct reuse at previously unseen sites. To address this scalability gap, this paper presents a domain-specific foundation-style pretraining framework that learns a shared forecasting model from heterogeneous multi-site and multi-horizon PV data. NWP-assisted forecasting is formulated as a sequence-completion problem that unifies historical PV power, meteorological observations, future numerical weather prediction (NWP) forecasts, and time encodings. Three pretraining objectives are designed and systematically evaluated, including forecast-aligned reconstruction, generic reconstruction, and mixed reconstruction. The mixed objective combines the operational structure of PV forecasting with random sequence reconstruction and provides the most balanced performance across forecasting accuracy, degraded NWP conditions, and downstream adaptation. Experiments on 479 distributed PV stations use 340 stations for model development and 139 spatially held-out stations for zero-shot evaluation. Across horizons from 6 hours to 7 days, the multi-site pretrained MR model outperforms representative station-specific and shared baselines, as well as generalist time-series models, including a recent large-scale pretrained foundation model. The pretrained model can also be directly deployed at new PV stations and subsequently adapted to region-specific operational objectives when local data become available, providing a scalable forecasting paradigm for large distributed PV fleets.


### [78] [Engram Memory Network: Brain-Inspired Prototype Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27770)
*Hyunjun Kim, Myoung Hoon Ha*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes decision-making. In response, prototype-based models in explainable AI have attracted growing attention for their ability to retrieve visually similar examples as faithful explanations. Although these models achieve competitive classification accuracy, they often exhibit degraded explanation quality across datasets with varying characteristics, limiting their practical applicability. We draw on insights from cognitive neuroscience, particularly the human memory system, which encodes input-relevant information into representations that support recognition. We propose an Engram Memory Network (EMN), a model that emulates the ventral visual stream and inferior temporal cortex in the human brain. Central to this model is a Hopfield-inspired memory module that iteratively retrieves representative prototypes. These memory-refined latents are used for both reconstruction and classification, encouraging the selected prototypes to capture input-relevant information across diverse datasets. To address the lack of standardized evaluation for prototype explanations, we assess three complementary aspects: perceptual similarity (LPIPS and DISTS) for perceived resemblance, subclass alignment, measuring whether prototypes match the input's fine-grained category beyond supervised labels, and information preservation (NMI), measuring how much input information is captured by selected prototypes. Experiments show that EMN improves visual similarity by 17.06%, subclass alignment by 104.57%, and NMI by 42.42% on average across CIFAR-10/100, MNIST, HAM10000, and CUB-200, while achieving the highest average classification accuracy (87.62%) among all methods. These findings underscore the effectiveness of neuroscience-inspired explanation mechanisms in advancing the interpretability and reliability of prototype-based models.


### [79] [How Much Do $\ell_1$ Attacks Need Concentrated Perturbations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27773)
*Melika Shirian, Kianoosh Vadaei, Arshia Hemmat*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A global $\ell_1$ budget limits how much an adversary may change an image, not how that change is distributed. We ask how much of an $\ell_1$ attack's observed success remains when large coordinate changes are restricted. We add a per-coordinate cap to the budget, enforce the joint constraint by exact in-loop projection, and sweep the cap for APGD-$\ell_1$, SLIDE, and FMN-$\ell_1$ without altering their objectives. Across 18 standard-model settings, caps of at most $32/255$ retain a median $97\%$ of attack success relative to each method's native uncapped baseline, while median peak magnitude falls by $80\%$ and median effective support nearly triples. At matched realized $\ell_1$, capped perturbations remain structurally distinct; separate ImageNet comparisons also show lower LPIPS and DISTS at selected caps. Direct in-loop constrained optimization preserves up to $88$ percentage points more attack success than post-hoc clipping, highlighting the importance of constraint enforcement. Suitable caps nevertheless vary across attacks and models, and adversarially trained classifiers exhibit more heterogeneous concentration sensitivity. These results establish perturbation concentration as a complementary, model- and attack-dependent axis of $\ell_1$ attack evaluation.


### [80] [Foundations of the Scaling Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27789)
*Tianyu Zhao, Zhixiang Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Empirical scaling laws relate validation loss to model size, training exposure and compute, providing a quantitative basis for allocating resources. This technical note explains these relationships through three linked analyses. Geometric approximation connects spatial resolution, intrinsic dimension and smoothness to function error; the loss function determines how that error transfers to task risk. Spectral analysis combines feature visibility, covariance eigenvalues and target alignment to describe how additional data reduce unresolved predictive mass. Constrained optimization then gives the parameter-data balance and the training-inference trade-off. We also examine emergence and grokking, separating scoring thresholds from learning dynamics, and test-time scaling, separating candidate coverage from selected-answer accuracy. Applications to vision and molecular prediction show how domain-specific coordinates change the resource comparison. An $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ case study quantifies the dimension dependence of quotient-space approximation gains and compares optimized compute frontiers, alongside separate analyses of conformational coverage and correlated-sample precision.


### [81] [SliMOO: Interpretable Multi-Objective Evolutionary Search for LLM Depth Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27812)
*Guanchen Li, Yixing Xu, Xuanwu Yin, Dong Li, Emad Barsoum*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) grow more powerful, their scale makes them costly to serve. Depth pruning offers a practical route to efficiency by directly reducing memory and latency without hardware-specific support. However, we observe that transformer layers exhibit markedly different task-dependent layer contributions, whereas existing depth-pruning methods ignore this heterogeneity and thus tend to optimize for a narrow domain or benchmark rather than preserve broad capability. This suggests that LLM depth pruning should be formulated as a multi-objective optimization problem rather than a single-score ranking problem. We propose SliMOO, an interpretable multi-objective evolutionary search framework for LLM depth pruning. SliMOO first uses single-layer removal probing to construct a task-layer importance map, which provides an interpretable view of task-shared and task-specific layers and serves as a search prior. It then performs a Pareto-aware evolutionary search for pruning masks, where candidates are generated under the guidance of the task-layer prior, scored by their deviation from the dense model on each task, and retained through NSGA-II environmental selection. Across the Llama-3.1 and Qwen3 model families, SliMOO consistently achieves stronger multi-task trade-offs than rule-based, greedy, and scalarized baselines. For example, on Llama-3.1-70B at 25% sparsity, it improves the math-domain average from 24.33% to 34.40% and the code-domain average from 35.03% to 39.14%. Code will be made available at this https URL .


### [82] [From Generic to Dedicated: A Novel Optimizer for Online Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27835)
*Yongyi Wu, Zheng Wang, Sen Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Online Continual Learning (OCL) requires models to learn from non-stationary data streams under a strict single-pass constraint, making them highly susceptible to catastrophic forgetting. While existing studies have explored various strategies from data, architecture, and optimization perspectives, the optimizer often directly uses generic approaches such as SGD and AdamW. In this work, we reveal a new link between optimization dynamics and OCL needs by recasting Polyak-Ruppert averaging as the engine of "plasticity" and Primal averaging as the anchor of "stability". This inspires our novel optimizer, SPIN (Stability-Plasticity INterpolation), which explicitly decouples plasticity and stability by interpolating between a fast-moving "plasticity" sequence and an adaptive "stability" sequence. We theoretically demonstrate that SPIN implicitly employs an inverse Hessian approximation, providing crucial Tikhonov regularization to damp noisy, single-sample Hessian estimates and mitigate forgetting. More importantly, SPIN can be seamlessly integrated into existing OCL methods by taking resulted gradients as input and replacing standard optimizers. Extensive experiments show that using SPIN instead of standard optimizers yields consistent and significant performance gains across multiple OCL approaches, including the challenging rehearsal-free setting and under strict memory constraints.


### [83] [Elicitation or Expansion? Demystifying Gains from Agent Harness Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27889)
*Younghyun Kim, Wooseok Seo, Minseo Kim, Seunghyuk Oh, Minjae Lee, Donghoon Kim, Hyung Il Koo, Jaehyung Kim, Wonjun Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent studies in harness optimization have shown substantial performance gains in language model agents without updating model weights. A well-designed harness more effectively leverages the underlying model's capabilities by shaping context, tool use, and execution workflows. However, naïve success rates alone do not fully characterize whether optimized harness yields more reliable success on tasks already within reach (elicitation) or a broader range of solvable tasks (expansion). This distinction is crucial for recursive self-improvement and evolutionary search, as expansion may provide a broader range of successful experiences to push the capability boundaries. Despite the growing significance, this distinction remains underexplored in harness optimization. We conduct a systematic analysis to study the effect of gains from harness optimization. Building on pass@k analyses in the reinforcement learning (RL) literature that compare models before and after RL training, we compare optimized harnesses with their pre-optimization counterparts. Specifically, we assess changes in reachable task coverage, compare harness optimization with model scaling and increased inference-time compute, and examine how gains vary with task difficulty. Across static prediction and dynamic tasks, we find that pass@1 advantages of harness over stronger interventions can reverse at larger attempt budgets and aggregate gains conceal shortfalls on hard tasks, underscoring the need for a fine-grained diagnosis.


### [84] [Physical-Variable-Informed Deep Learning for Robust Diagnosis in Industrial Fluid Transport Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27891)
*Yu Sha, Junqi Tao, Dixin Zhou, Horst Stoecker, Domagoj Vnucec, Andreas Widl, Kai Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Industrial fluid transport systems are monitored through heterogeneous sensing streams, but robust fault diagnosis remains difficult because response signals vary with operating conditions and differ from physical variables in sampling rate, observation duration and data granularity. To address these challenges, we propose a physical-variable-informed deep learning framework (PhyNet) for robust fault diagnosis under real-world sampling heterogeneity. PhyNet treats physical variables as global operating constraints and response signals as local diagnostic evidence. Its physical-to-response cross-attention mechanism connects sequence-level physical-variable tokens with a learnable global response token, enabling interaction without strict temporal alignment. Across four benchmarks, including three real-world cavitation datasets and one public two-phase-flow dataset, PhyNet achieves the best performance across all evaluated metrics. Class-wise analyses, together with attention and masking analyses, show improved discrimination of ambiguous states and indicate that physical variables meaningfully constrain local response interpretation. These findings demonstrate a deployment-oriented approach to robust diagnosis in heterogeneous industrial environments.


### [85] [The Shape of the DPO Hyperparameter Landscape: What to Measure, How It Moves with Scale, and When Not to Transfer It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27895)
*Hannan Mahadik, David Salinas, Jacek Golebiowski, Aaron Klein*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Direct preference optimisation (DPO) is widely used for language model post-training, but its performance depends strongly on the choice of its hyperparameters, such as the learning rate and the reference-policy regularisation parameter $\beta$. To better understand the DPO hyperparameter landscape, we conduct extensive experiments using a dense hyperparameter grid across different model scales from the same family. This allows us to analyse how the optimal DPO hyperparameters evolve with model size and the number of update steps, with intermediate checkpoints providing an inexpensive signal to accelerate the hyperparameter optimisation process. Furthermore, by evaluating our models on both static and dynamic benchmarks, we identify which benchmarks provide the strongest signal for hyperparameter selection. Finally, we investigate how different state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimisation strategies perform in this setting. Our experiments show that the optimal learning rate varies with model size and that hyperparameters are not transferable across training datasets. We also find that validation loss is a poor proxy for hyperparameter optimisation, as hyperparameters that minimize validation loss only weakly correlate with optimal hyperparameters for downstream benchmarks. Therefore, DPO hyperparameters need to be tuned specifically for the target model, dataset, and evaluation objective. We also show that short candidate training runs can substantially reduce the cost of this selection. We release all our training configurations and evaluation results to support reproducible analysis of selection quality and training cost.


### [86] [Near-Duplicate Image Selection Transfers Preferences in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27943)
*Jaeyoung Kim, Eunseok Kim, Dongsuk Jang, Seunghyun Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Subliminal learning is the phenomenon in which a model transmits its preferences to another model through data that do not explicitly express them. As models increasingly curate training data for other models, we ask whether this transfer can arise from choosing among near-duplicate images while keeping the source photographs and human-written captions fixed. For each photograph, a curator selects one of eight near-duplicate variants by caption likelihood, without target-specific prompts. Students trained on target-preferring curators' selections show higher mean target-versus-alternatives log-likelihood ratios in an image-free readout than students trained on neutral selections. Under a prespecified decision rule, this directional shift appears for an owl target in LLaVA-1.5, Qwen2.5-VL, Qwen3-VL, and Gemma 4, with curators and students initialized from the same base checkpoint, and target rotations move it toward camels, oak trees, and a political figure. The owl target-selected student also scores above all count-matched random-reselection students in three models and above 55 of 60 in LLaVA-1.5, and Qwen3-VL shows the shift again on a new source bank with newly trained curators. Its size depends on dataset size, learning rate, and training budget; greedy choices among the eight options move toward the target by 10.4 percentage points in Qwen3-VL and 0.5-1.3 points elsewhere. First-generation students also pass the owl preference to a second generation through captions without explicit owl mentions in LLaVA-1.5 and Qwen3-VL. Thus even a deduplication-style choice among near-identical copies can shift students' target-relative readouts with source photographs and captions fixed; the tested linear probes classify the selecting arm with accuracies up to 59.7% (chance 50%).


### [87] [On Johnson-Lindenstrauss Transforms for Byzantine-Robust Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27989)
*Aleksandr Shestakov, Vladimir Aletov, Aleksandr Beznosikov*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The growing scale of data and models in machine learning calls for efficient optimization methods. Distributed optimization addresses this challenge, with compression mechanisms helping to reduce communication costs. A classical tool for dimensionality reduction is the Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) transform, which is simple to implement and offers strong geometric guarantees. In particular, JL transforms approximately preserve inner products and pairwise distances between vectors with high probability. In this paper, we exploit this property in Byzantine-robust optimization, where worker messages are weighted according to their alignment with a trusted reference gradient. We further support our theoretical findings with experimental results.


### [88] [When Momentum Helps Polyak--Ruppert Averaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27990)
*Alexandra Suvroikova, Darina Dvinskikh, Yuriy Dorn, Alexander Gasnikov*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Polyak--Ruppert averaging gives SGD and momentum methods the same asymptotic covariance. We characterize how fast finite runs reach it and how far they can be parallelized. For strongly convex quadratics with additive noise, we prove an online--offline bridge: the empirical risk minimizer on the samples of a tail average is one Newton step from the tail-averaged query point of any online procedure. For SGD, heavy ball, and Nesterov's method with constant momentum, this step is a boundary term at the two ends of the tail and splits the gap to the offline estimator into center, covariance, and shape errors. Necessary and sufficient conditions show that, on the least curved direction, the center error sets the pace: SGD needs $m\gg\kappa^2$ rounds but momentum only $m\gg\kappa$, where $\kappa$ is the condition number, so at sample size $N$ the critical batch size grows from $\Theta(\sqrt{N}/\kappa)$ to $\Theta(\sqrt{N/\kappa})$. The covariance error separates the momentum methods: heavy ball has relative variance excess $\Theta(\kappa^{3/2}/m)$ along the stiffest direction, whereas Nesterov has relative error $O(\sqrt{\kappa}/m)$ in every direction. The shape error is method-independent by a Berry--Esseen bound uniform in $\kappa$ and batch size, so confidence intervals are valid when the other two errors vanish. We further examine how the conclusions change under heavy-tailed or state-dependent noise and clipping, and establish a first-order comparison with ERM for nonlinear Nesterov averaging under explicit conditions.


### [89] [RANGER: Retrieval Agent for Navigating Graphs with Semantic Evaluation Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28010)
*Artem Mikhaylov, Pavel Konoplev, Nikolay Efanov, Artyom Sorokin, Evgeny Burnaev*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models and Reinforcement Learning offers a promising direction for complex sequential decision-making tasks, such as multi-hop question answering over knowledge graphs. While LLMs provide strong semantic understanding and reasoning capabilities, they often struggle with precise, multi-step navigation in structured environments. Conversely, RL agents excel at learning navigation policies but are hindered by the difficulty of designing semantically meaningful reward functions. This paper introduces a novel hybrid approach in which an LLM serves as a mentor to an RL agent during training by providing dense, semantically informed reward signals. The RL agent, built on a Transformer-based policy and trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization, learns to navigate a knowledge graph to retrieve relevant information. The LLM evaluates the semantic relevance of the agent's traversed paths, generating reward signals that align the agent's behavior with the information needs expressed in a query. We integrate this Retrieval Agent for Navigating Graphs with Semantic Evaluation Rewards (RANGER) into a complete question-answering pipeline and evaluate it on the open question answering datasets. Experimental results show that the proposed approach outperforms both a baseline retrieval-augmented system with classical graph search and one using an RL agent trained with heuristic rewards, achieving a significant increase in NLP metrics and LLM-as-a-Judge scores. Our findings demonstrate that deep integration of LLMs into the RL training loop yields more effective and semantically aligned retrieval policies for complex reasoning tasks.


### [90] [From RL Papers to Framework Code: Inclusion and Review in Seven Open-Source Training Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28027)
*Taiheng Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Practitioners use reinforcement learning methods for language models through training frameworks, so a method that a framework includes is one configuration change away for its users. We measure how often this happens. We trace seven open-source frameworks for RL post-training through their commit histories and link them to 6,939 screened arXiv papers from January 2025 to June 2026. Of 1,598 papers that propose a general RL training method, 37 reached at least one framework. Across five quarterly cohorts of papers, the number included within 180 days stayed between 5 and 7 while the cohorts grew from 72 to 386 papers. Compared with papers from the same quarters, included papers more often trained policies of 30B parameters or more, at 41 against 10 percent, and more often changed a coefficient, clip, mask or weight in the existing loss, at 29 against 9 percent. Both gaps hold without papers from the companies that maintain the frameworks, while code release was equally common in both groups. The frameworks' issues and pull requests show that few methods are turned away in public. More than nine in ten of the general methods no framework included have no thread there that cites or names them, and many proposals that do appear stay open without a reply. Two further records, the frameworks' issues and the technical reports of 13 large open-weight models, show that users rarely report training failures in public and that the reports state 8 of the 13 widely included methods. We release the data as a tracker that links training failures to framework implementations and lists each framework's contribution rules and earlier proposals.


### [91] [When Does Training on Synthetic Data Provably Work?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28036)
*Leroy Odunlami, Tobias Riedlinger, Hanno Gottschalk*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: High-quality training data are a major bottleneck in machine learning, motivating increasing use of artificially generated data. We study when a binary classifier trained on synthetic data transfers successfully to a real distribution, under a shared conditional label distribution. First, we derive finite-sample excess-error bounds when the real covariate distribution is absolutely continuous with respect to the synthetic distribution under a controlled density ratio. Second, we study the partially and fully singular case, where the real distribution has mass outside the synthetic one. If the hypothesis class is sufficiently expressive, synthetic risk alone cannot uniformly control real-world error: a classifier can be nearly optimal on synthetic data while approaching worst-case error on the uncovered real region, even when the distributions are arbitrarily close in Wasserstein distance. For function classes where hypotheses and the log-odds of the conditional label probability are Lipschitz, we recover a bound in terms of the probability that a real sample lies outside the reach of the synthetic data, restoring a transfer guarantee even in the fully singular case. Experiments confirm this in controlled low-dimensional settings and on 32,200 images generated as counterparts to real ImageNet images by four state-of-the-art generators: an adversarial classifier reaches 0.5% error on the synthetic domain but 99.25% error on the real domain, while ordinarily trained architectures show positively correlated synthetic and real test errors. Synthetic data should be budgeted at high sample counts and models validated on real data.


### [92] [Poisoning-Based Backdoors Are Not Cryptographic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28045)
*Masahiro Kaneko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: To defend against backdoor attacks on neural networks, the defender must identify the verifier function that determines whether an input is a trigger, rather than rejecting individual triggers in isolation, since an attacker can generate new triggers satisfying the same verifier faster than they can be blocked. If a backdoor verifier could be installed with the asymmetry of a cryptographic authentication scheme, in which only the attacker can produce trigger inputs while the defender cannot reverse-engineer the verifier from observations, learning-based defense would be impossible in principle. We show that no such asymmetry can arise from poisoning-based installation, in which the only verifier-dependent input to training is a poisoned dataset. The argument is a reduction. A training procedure that installs a verifier from poisoned data, so that the backdoor fires on unseen triggers while benign behavior is preserved, is itself a learner for that verifier. Any verifier that is computationally hard to learn is therefore impossible to install through poisoning, and any verifier that poisoning can install is reconstructible by a white-box defender holding comparably many labeled trigger observations. A complementary argument shows that a secret key supplied to the model at inference is observable to the same defender. The result takes representative trigger observations as given and concerns when the backdoor fires rather than what it does. Experiments on instruction-tuned large language models confirm the dichotomy, with no configuration yielding a backdoor that is both effective for the attacker and irrecoverable for the defender.


### [93] [Universal Online Convex Optimization With Delayed Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28058)
*Hao Qiu, Andrew Jacobsen, Emmanuel Esposito, Mengxiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study universal online convex optimization with delayed feedback. We develop a single algorithm that simultaneously adapts to general convex, strongly convex, and exp-concave losses without prior knowledge of the curvature class or parameters. Our regret bounds match the best known dependence on the time horizon $T$ and delays up to doubly logarithmic factors: roughly $\sqrt{T + d_{\mathrm{tot}}}$ for convex losses and $\sigma_{\max}\log T$ rates with curved losses, where $d_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is the total cumulative delay and $\sigma_{\max}$ is the maximum number of simultaneously missing observations. Our main technical contribution is a delayed meta-learner with a second-order excess-loss guarantee, which enables curvature-adaptive aggregation of delayed base learners. In addition, our new exp-concave base learner yields a sharper regret bound which improves the state-of-the-art delay dependence by replacing the maximum delay with the potentially much smaller $\sigma_{\max}$, while retaining the complementary $\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{tot}}}$ dependence.


### [94] [MuPACE: Learning to Pace Muon for LoRA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28072)
*Jan Kobiolka, Christian Frey, Josif Grabocka*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Learning rate schedules are typically fixed before training begins, yet when fine-tuning large language models with low-rank adapters (LoRA), the appropriate scale varies substantially with the model, task, and adapter configuration. Practitioners re-tune a schedule for every new problem, yet a tuned schedule still cannot react to the training dynamics it encounters. We instead amortize learning rate selection across LoRA fine-tuning problems. We leave the base optimizer, Muon, untouched, so that its normalized update fixes the direction, and meta-learn only the global step size. Our method MuPACE is a small recurrent policy, meta-trained across a distribution of fine-tuning runs, that conditions on the observed trajectory and emits bounded adjustments to an interpretable analytic schedule. On 64 fine-tuning problems, MuPACE outperforms standard fixed schedules at a strong shared default on 64-78% of problems and is on par with the same schedules with per-task tuned peaks. Meta-trained only on models with up to 16 layers, MuPACE transfers one-shot to unseen tasks on models as deep as 64 layers, such as Qwen3-32B and Qwen3.8-27B, where a single run remains competitive with swept schedules.


### [95] [From Complex Histories to Simple Predictors: Predictive Dimension in Dynamic Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28090)
*Hantong Feng, Duxin Chen, Wenwu Yu*

Main category: cs.LG
