Random-token replacement, an input-corruption augmentation, is one remedy reported to lower validation loss, but a lower loss alone does not reveal which source of error it reduces. On a controlled source whose entropy rate is known in closed form, we show that replacement shrinks predictions toward the corpus average. We also introduce a decomposition that separates validation loss into approximation, estimation, and memorization terms. This shrinkage view predicts that replacement's bias cost needs no memorization but its benefit does. Memorization raises validation loss after its minimum, so we report the effect at two points, the minimum validation loss and the end of a fixed budget. Where replacement helps, it reduces this rise by about 60%. At the end of the budget, it turns from harmful to helpful as the controlled source becomes less predictable; at the minimum, it helps at no predictability level under the fixed recipe. On Korean and Japanese web text, replacement removes about a third of the loss rise after the minimum by the end of the budget, but its effect at the minimum is much smaller. Across ten real corpora, replacement lowers the final loss on nine. It harms only the census corpus, program-generated JSON that is the most redundant of the ten and much more redundant than any of 64 further prose and code corpora we measure.


### [421] [WORTH Benchmark: Evidence Discovery under Not-Fully-Cooperative Information -- A Two-Episode Pilot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27597)
*Bo Zou, Chao Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Existing reasoning evaluations typically provide task-relevant information to models as available premises, rarely examining concurrently how models discover evidence autonomously and how information presentation within narratives guides or obscures judgment. This paper proposes the WORTH Benchmark: models read original mystery narratives without clue annotations, autonomously select key clue sentences, identify case participants, and explain their reasoning process. The narrative materials are designed by professional authors such that evidence facilitating reasoning coexists with narrative arrangements that delay the revelation of truth. We report a pilot experiment under full-story conditions across two episodes, conducting a total of 70 formal runs on models, of which 67 runs are content-scorable. Scoring separately records hits on author-designated key clue sentences and participant selections; natural language explanations are used for case diagnosis and are not scored independently. The results show that clue hits and participant judgments are not synchronized; across 37 content-scorable runs in Episode 02, 31 runs simultaneously selected the same set of incorrect participants, while adjacent suspicion-oriented and exoneration-oriented sentences were selected 25 times and 0 times, respectively. These phenomena illustrate the diagnostic value of component-wise analysis and suggest that not-fully-cooperative informational relationships may influence evidence selection and judgment. This study only implements full-story conditions for two episodes, and does not establish stable model rankings or infer general mechanisms on this basis; human player records have not yet been cleaned, and this version does not report human-model comparisons.


### [422] [ABench: Adversarily Benchmarking Large Language Models across Taxonomized Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27611)
*Liping Yang, Yongqi Leng, Jingting Zheng, Yuqi Ren, Shaolin Zhu, Deyi Xiong*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Ensuring the alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) with diverse cultural values is critical for their safe global deployment. However, existing alignment benchmarks are predominantly English-centric or confined to simple discriminative tasks. Moreover, they often fail to effectively evaluate model robustness against complex, adversarial inductions within specific cultural contexts. To address these limitations, we propose ABench, a fine-grained, handcrafted benchmark specifically tailored to taxonomized values in Chinese. Grounded in a rigorous hierarchical taxonomy comprising 158 derived value subcategories, ABench consists of 2,895 open-ended questions. These questions employ techniques such as role-playing and logical traps to probe the stability of models' stances in adversarially induced contexts. Furthermore, we propose a weighted automated evaluation framework that utilizes question-specific scoring criteria for nuanced assessment. Extensive experiments on 16 mainstream LLMs reveal that most models exhibit significant vulnerabilities when subjected to sophisticated adversary attacks on values (scoring below 60). This underscores the need for more robust, culturally specific alignment strategies. Our data will be publicly released to promote further research on taxonomized value alignment.


### [423] [Corpus Quality Improves, Retrieval Utility Does Not: An Ablation of Preprocessing and Refinement in a Speech-to-QA Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27652)
*Wonsup Shin, Jingu Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Practitioners building training corpora with a pipeline of language models keep a preprocessing or refinement (post-process) stage when switching it on raises the corpus quality. We ablate a production pipeline that turns conference speech into question-answer pairs. The preprocessing stage rewrites the transcript, and the refinement stage filters the generated pairs. Switching each on and off gives four corpora from the same 40 Korean medical and finance sessions. On the corpus itself, quality rises as the stages switch on. Four LLM judges and six human domain experts rank the four corpora the same way, and the fully refined corpus leads by +0.18 and +0.22 on a 1-5 scale. We then ask whether that gain carries downstream. We supply each corpus to six models at inference and measure retrieval accuracy. The ranking does not hold. Enabling the transcript rewrite lowers retrieval accuracy by 0.061, the only stage effect that survives correction for multiple comparisons. That stage also reads the whole session, so it almost doubles the cost of one. With both stages on, accuracy does not change. An equivalence test we declared in advance confirms this. The corpus itself is clearly valuable. It lifts retrieval accuracy from 0.231 to 0.748 in every model and domain we test. More than a third of its pairs carry knowledge that neither of two frontier models produces on its own. On our data, the null does not come from weak corpora. A rising score on a rubric of this kind did not predict what the corpus holds for a model.


### [424] [Beyond Calibration: Temporal LLM Confidence Assessment Across Languages and Local Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27702)
*Sahil Kale*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language models are expected to estimate their own uncertainty, with confidence signals used to guide retrieval, adaptive compute allocation, routing, and selective abstention. We investigate whether confidence signals measured at different temporal stages of problem solving encode distinct forms of epistemic information and remain reliable beyond English-centric settings. Inspired by classical meta-memory theory, we distinguish anticipatory pre-solve confidence (Feeling-of-Knowing, FOK) from retrospective post-solve confidence (Judgement-of-Learning, JOL). We evaluate these signals across two frontier LLMs, multiple reasoning domains, translated Marathi benchmarks, and a newly constructed benchmark of genuinely local Marathi factual questions. Across settings, the cheaper pre-solve signal remains weakly calibrated, while post-solve confidence generally provides stronger epistemic alignment despite its additional inference cost. Confidence behaviour transfers surprisingly well under direct language translation, with calibration trends largely preserved between English and Marathi, but degrades substantially on local Marathi knowledge, suggesting that linguistic transfer alone is insufficient for region-specific information needs. Finally, hallucinated responses maintain persistently elevated confidence after answer generation, highlighting important limitations of current LLM self-assessment mechanisms.


### [425] [Hearsay or Fact? How Framing and Elicitation Shape LLM Probability Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27745)
*An-Sheng Jhang*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judgment assistants and as stand-in participants in psychology experiments, yet little is known about how much their probability estimates move in response to a single new piece of information. We pair premise-hypothesis items from $\delta$-SNLI with one strengthener and one weakener each, elicit the prior and the posteriors in separate calls, and measure the directional movement s: the mean posterior change signed by the annotated direction, so that the prior cancels out. We vary whether the update is stated ("Here is something else you learned") or reported ("Someone said that"), and whether the model gives one probability or five weighted estimates. We test seven configurations: two local models (Qwen3-8B, OLMo-2-7B; 240 pairs) and five configurations run through agent command-line harnesses (120 pairs), with hypotheses and analysis rules fixed before the formal runs (not publicly registered). Under stated, direct elicitation all seven move in the annotated direction (s = 18-28 percentage points). Reporting lowers s in all seven point estimates (significantly in six, by 2-5 points) and raises the share of reverse movements in all seven: the effect is consistent but small. Five weighted estimates lower the share of responses at $\leq$5% or $\geq$95% in all seven, but in the two local models they also reduce s by 43% and 68%, whereas in the harnessed configurations the point estimates of the change lie between -3.7 and +0.5 points. An elicitation format that reduces extreme values thus need not preserve sensitivity to evidence; the two should be tested separately. Because $\delta$-NLI annotates only direction, we make no claim about how large an update should be. Code, data and the frozen plan: this https URL


### [426] [SMART: Semantic Segment Mapping and Assessment of Recognition Transcripts with Contextual Rubrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27820)
*Xiaoyu Gu, Chencheng Wang, Fuyang Li, Yu Xi, Tao Liu, Jiaoqi Guo, Kai Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Word error rate (WER) assigns equal cost to lexical edits despite their different semantic consequences, while overall semantic scores cannot identify which individual errors alter the intended meaning. We formulate ASR semantic evaluation as context-aware, segment-level consequence assessment and propose Semantic Segment Mapping and Assessment of Recognition Transcripts with Contextual Rubrics (SMART), a framework for traceable semantic evaluation and targeted ASR correction. SMART maps reference-hypothesis discrepancies into coherent segments and applies a structured contextual rubric to assign explicit consequence labels. These labels support Semantic Impact Error Rate (SIER), which quantifies consequential errors at utterance, chunk, and lexical levels. Against majority-vote human labels, SMART achieves 0.843 F1 versus 0.745 for direct LLM judgment. Evaluation across multiple LLMs further demonstrates that SMART is relatively insensitive to the choice of evaluation model, while MER and SIER reveal different aspects of ASR quality. A separate target-reference-free rubric guides correction using the target hypothesis and surrounding ASR hypotheses, outperforming a matched w/o Rubric baseline on both datasets, with further absolute reductions of 0.85-0.98 percentage points in MER and 1.18-1.65 percentage points in SIER_lex.


### [427] [CollabSciReM: Aligning Language Models for Scientific Conversations via Science-Aware Reward Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27901)
*Sasi Kiran Gaddipati, Gollam Rabby, Sören Auer*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as research collaborators for hypothesis refinement, methodological planning, and technical analysis across multi-turn interactions. In this setting, a useful assistant must be accurate, methodologically justified, logically consistent, and token-efficient. Current alignment approaches rarely achieve all of these together. Instruction-tuned models gain conversational quality through verbosity, and we show that science-agnostic general collaborative rewards compress responses at the expense of scientific rigor. We propose CollabSciReM, a goal-conditioned reward framework that decomposes the session-level reward into conversational quality, scientific rigor, and structural efficiency. We also introduce SciQuality, a pairwise evaluation of multi-turn research conversations grounded in 300 held-out scientific papers and scored on six dimensions: methodological soundness, justification, logical consistency, factual correctness, novelty, and responsiveness. Fine-tuned on 8.62K synthetic research conversations, CollabSciReM is preferred on SciQuality over a science-agnostic reward baseline trained on the same data for all four Llama and Qwen models, including a 65.3% win rate at matched response length. For the 7B and 8B models, CollabSciReM outperforms the science-agnostic reward baseline on the primary metric in 8 of 10 single- and multi-turn benchmark settings. In a blind study, domain experts rated CollabSciReM significantly higher on novelty than the general-reward variant and reported fewer hallucinated responses than both the general-reward variant and the base model.


### [428] [Polarity Blindness in LLM Safety: A Systematic Study of Negation, Antonym Substitution, and Adversarial Framing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27908)
*Diego Miguel Lozano, Erik Derner, Juan Antonio Pérez-Ortiz, Nuria Oliver*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Negation remains a persistent challenge for language models across NLP tasks and modalities. We show that this weakness can undermine LLM safety guardrails through a structural vulnerability we term polarity blindness: the failure to recognize harmful intent after polarity-altering linguistic transformations. We study two forms of polarity inversion: grammatical negation and antonym replacement, and use synonym replacement as a polarity-preserving control. We further test whether an adversarial textual wrapper amplifies these failures. Using a 260-prompt benchmark across 10 state-of-the-art LLMs and 3 languages (English, Spanish, Czech), stratified by harmfulness potential and evaluated on a validated 0-5 harm scale, we find that polarity blindness affects most evaluated models, with mean harm scores reaching 4.12 for the most vulnerable model. The effect persists across typologically differing negation systems, suggesting that polarity blindness reflects a broader limitation in semantic robustness rather than a language- or grammar-specific failure.


### [429] [On-Policy Transition Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28048)
*Runyang You, Yongqi Li, Tiezheng Yu, Wenjie Li*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) consolidates expert capabilities into a single student by matching teacher and student final-layer distributions on student-generated rollouts. We investigate whether depthwise prediction transitions, the changes in predictions between layers, can provide additional task-relevant supervision. Our diagnostic study finds that stronger teacher--student agreement in intermediate transitions is associated with higher answer correctness within questions, with correct responses exhibiting lower mean transition KL across all measured intermediate layer pairs. These observations motivate On-Policy Transition Distillation (OPTD), which augments final-layer distribution matching by aligning teacher and student layer-to-layer log-probability increments on student-generated rollouts. Evaluations across three backbones and eight benchmarks spanning mathematics, coding, and instruction following show that OPTD achieves the best or tied-best overall performance in both dual- and three-domain distillation. In the three-domain setting, OPTD recovers $\approx 90\%$ of the overall teacher--student capability gap, compared with $\approx 75\%$ for OPD.


### [430] [Same Judgment, Different Yes: Measuring Multilingual Judgment Profiles in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28066)
*Haonan Huang, Yuwei Lu, Chengkun Cai*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Multilingual evaluations often score a model's opening yes or no, a token that also carries model and language answering conventions. We ask when different ways of eliciting and reading an answer recover the same judgment, and when they measure an answer habit. We collect 1.1 million replies from eight models in six languages on up to 105 moral vignettes, varying the question's form, the answer constraint, and the reading of the reply, token or sentence, in both wordings (the FAR-6 battery). Story-level judgment profiles converge across the tested readouts and question forms, except for the yes/no token after a negative interrogative read under a preset mapping: on the same replies, token and sentence profiles agree at a median r = 0.67 across cells, while sentence profiles agree with free-sentence profiles at 0.94, a known linguistic variability. With each language's story consensus removed, a model's profile is nearest to its own in another language for 39 of 48 model-language cells under proposition labels and 15 under free sentences, against 5 expected by chance. Particle, position, and named-term preferences follow their own patterns. A model can keep its judgment and change its yes: scoring that token requires establishing which proposition it affirms.


### [431] [BAIT: Can LLMs Find What Biases a Reader? A Benchmark for Cognitive Bias Triggers in News](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28092)
*Yousra Fettach, Tijl De Bie*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Persuasive writing exploits cognitive biases, recruiting a reader's mental shortcuts to shape belief, often without the reader noticing. We call the linguistic features exploiting such mental shortcuts cognitive bias triggers. As large language models (LLMs) increasingly mediate how people encounter news, summarizing it, ranking it, and answering questions over it, a natural question is whether they can recognize the features of a text that bias a human reader. We introduce BAIT (Bias Annotation In Text), a resource for detecting cognitive bias triggers in news. BAIT pairs a schema of nine triggers, each specified in an annotator codebook, with 50 English news articles annotated by trained annotators at the article and span levels and adjudicated into a gold standard. On this data we define two tasks, detecting which triggers a text contains and locating the spans that carry them. We then benchmark six frontier LLMs zero-shot and with three or ten in-context examples. The models approach human agreement on which triggers an article contains, but rarely recover its full set of triggers, and they reach less than half of human agreement in locating the passages that carry them. Moreover, they over-predict triggers that come with easy surface cues, and most of the passages they mark for these triggers are not triggers, yet carry the trigger's surface cue far more often than random text. These findings suggest that the evaluated LLMs respond to surface cues rather than to the mechanisms that make a passage manipulative. We release the schema, the codebook, standoff annotations, and the evaluation pipeline.


### [432] [Africa-Papers: A 10-Billion-Token Multi-Domain Academic Corpus for African Foundation Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28173)
*Mich-Seth Owusu*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) depend fundamentally on the diversity, depth, and factual rigor of their pre-training corpora. However, contemporary foundational pre-training datasets (such as The Pile, S2ORC, and Dolma) exhibit a severe structural omission: African scientific, medical, legal, and agricultural scholarship constitutes less than 0.5% of global pre-training tokens. Consequently, state-of-the-art models suffer from systematic failure modes when reasoning about tropical epidemiology, localized smallholder agro-ecologies, regional economic governance, and indigenous linguistic structures. To resolve this representation deficit and advance African intellectual sovereignty in artificial intelligence, we introduce Africa-Papers, the largest curated multi-domain Pan-African academic pre-training corpus to date. Comprising over 844,807 full-text papers spanning 10.15 billion tokens across 879 Parquet shards, Africa-Papers unifies four primary scientific streams: (1) continental peer-reviewed journals indexed in DOAJ across 31 African nations, (2) the CGIAR CGSpace agricultural repository, (3) African-authored and African-focused STEM/AI preprints on arXiv, and (4) open-access international publications by scholars affiliated with African institutions indexed via OpenAlex. The entire corpus is processed into layout-aware, clean Markdown preserving section hierarchies, mathematical equations, and tabular data, accompanied by exact BPE token counts and a standardized 8-domain taxonomy. We release Africa-Papers openly on the Hugging Face Hub under the AfriSpeech organization to accelerate continual pre-training and domain evaluation for foundation models globally.


### [433] [Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176)
*Jiapu Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. Although RAG and Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) provide practical ways to enhance LLMs, they remain insufficient for temporal reasoning. Existing retrieval methods typically prioritize semantic relevance while overlooking temporal validity, event order, and evolutionary dependencies, resulting in evidence that is relevant in isolation but temporally fragmented. Meanwhile, conventional PEFT methods usually employ fixed and input-agnostic low-rank updates, making it difficult to capture time-dependent entity semantics and relation drift. To address these limitations, we propose Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment (TEPCA), a framework for LLM reasoning over TKGs. TEPCA aligns LLM reasoning with temporal knowledge at both the evidence and parameter levels. At the evidence level, it introduces time-constrained Dynamic Relevance-Guided Path (DRGP) pruning to preserve globally coherent temporal paths, together with Mutual-Information (MI)-based pruning to retain query-informative local facts under temporal constraints. At the parameter level, it integrates Dynamic-Gated LoRA (DG-LoRA), a timestamp-conditioned low-rank adaptation mechanism that dynamically modulates the effective adaptation subspace according to temporal context. This design enables LLMs to better capture fine-grained entity evolution and time-dependent relation semantics while preserving parameter efficiency. Experimental results on two TKG benchmarks demonstrate that TEPCA achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional evaluations of DG-LoRA on eight commonsense QA datasets further verify its general input-adaptive capacity, scalability across LLM backbones, and interpretability.


### [434] [Beyond a Single Sentiment Score: Topical and Affective Dimensions of Emoji Meaning on Twitter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28337)
*Rohan James, Ioannis Christofilogiannis, Yuuv Jauhari, Shreya Shah, Gauri Pethkar*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Emoji are widely treated as compact affective tokens whose meaning is summarised by a single sentiment score. We argue that this picture is incomplete. From a corpus of 16{,}575 English tweets and a per-emoji metric we call \emph{stability delta} (within-class minus between-class cosine similarity of sentence embeddings), we show that emojis used in tightly clustered topical contexts also exhibit \emph{more} polarised crowd-sourced sentiment, not less. Validated against the Kralj Novak et al.\ (2015) sentiment lexicon, the central correlation is Spearman $\rho = +0.361$ (Holm $p = 0.015$, 95\% bootstrap CI $[+0.10, +0.59]$); against the independent Ferré et al.\ (2025) Spanish 13-emotion ratings, $\rho = +0.509$ ($p = 0.0003$). The result survives permutation testing, BCa bootstrap, leave-one-out jackknife, partial correlation controlling for emoji frequency, and Holm correction across a four-test family. Topical coherence and affective dispersion are distinct dimensions of emoji meaning. As a downstream check we report leakage-free five-fold cross-validation results for sarcasm classification on the Emoji Sarcasm Dataset; all three baselines (a gated multimodal architecture, fine-tuned BERTweet features, and fine-tuned RoBERTa features) score above 0.97 macro F1, indicating the dataset has saturating emoji-label cues. We frame the classifier as supporting evidence rather than the contribution.


### [435] [The Instruction Tax on Factual Recall: Base Models Can Beat Their Instruction-Tuned Counterparts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28407)
*Christophe Khalil, Aya Mourad*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: We present our solution to the AKBC LM-KBC 2026 Shared Task, where we achieved a score of 0.6836 on the official leaderboard, more than double the baseline of 0.2964. This task requires generating all possible objects for subject-relation pairs using a language model with up to 32 billion parameters, with no retrieval or fine-tuning involved. We show that the prompting methodology is as important as the model architecture: while the instruction-tuned model version achieves 0.5681 with its chat interface, the base model achieves 0.6836 when prompted using text completion. Thus, instruction tuning decreases factual recall; we refer to this effect as the instruction tax, which is caused by the model's weights rather than the prompting method. Our final solution is fairly straightforward: we prompt the base model with some examples from the training data, generate 64--256 outputs, and retain only the repeated ones. Two specialized methods are then applied for each relation: one improves the recall of large answer sets, and the other verifies a person's vitality before predicting their place of death.


### [436] [ProVe: Proposer-Verifier Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27815)
*Ziqi Jin, Weiwen Xu, Jiaxi Li, Yujie Zhong, Qi She, Aixin Sun*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive (AR) language models by letting a small model draft tokens serially and a large model verify them in parallel. We reverse these roles. Starting from a standard AR checkpoint, we fine-tune the large model into a proposer that predicts a block of future tokens in a single forward pass, then use one additional forward pass to verify the proposed block. This gives ProVe, Proposer-Verifier decoding. The verifier can be a smaller AR model or the proposer itself; the latter gives ProVe-lossless, which reproduces the proposer's greedy AR decoding. Block proposals already provide substantial room for acceleration: with a block size of 16, the proposed block has an average AR-matching prefix of 6.6 tokens on GSM8K. Across six benchmarks with a 32B proposer, ProVe-lossless reaches 2.5x average AR throughput without training a separate draft head. With a small 3B verifier, ProVe reaches 2.7x average throughput at 2.6 accuracy points below AR on the six-task average, providing a controllable tradeoff between speed and accuracy.


### [437] [Adaptive Replay Refinement: Allocating Test-Time Compute Between Revision and Resampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28294)
*Ahsan Bilal, Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin, Muhammad Umer, Ayesha Mohsin, Sarmad Bilal, Dean F. Hougen*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Test-time scaling allocates additional inference compute to refining existing solutions or generating new ones. This raises a fundamental question: Should I keep improving this solution, or stop working on it and try a completely new solution? Existing methods make this decision using fixed refinement schedules, self-verification judgments, or learned controllers, which can waste compute or rely on signals that may be incorrect or require additional training. We introduce Adaptive Replay Refinement (ARR), a training-free method that detects when a stationary revision reaches a fixed point and reallocates the released compute to a new independent sample; replay decoding treats the current solution as a draft and verifies unchanged tokens in parallel, enabling efficient fixed-point detection. Across seven models from five families and seven benchmarks, ARR matches majority voting on three of five mathematical reasoning benchmarks at equal compute, while reducing critical-path serial passes by up to 2.4 times. On MATH500, ARR achieves 84.6% accuracy with only 41% of the serial passes required by majority voting. These results demonstrate that detecting refinement exhaustion enables adaptive reallocation from revision to independent sampling, improving inference efficiency while highlighting the limits of self-revision. Code is available at this https URL .


### [438] [Every Model Leaves a Trace: Multi-Resolution LLM Fingerprinting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28434)
*Mohammad Reza Modarres, Armin Tourajmehr, Artin Tavasoli, Yadollah Yaghoobzadeh, Mohammad Taher Pilehvar*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language models leave model-specific traces in their generated text, but it remains unclear how finely these fingerprints can distinguish related models and which signals remain informative as obvious cues are reduced. We study this through fingerprint resolution, considering both model resolution, from different families to distilled and adapted variants, and signal resolution across behavioral, stylometric, lexical, and semantic evidence. We progressively suppress surface cues, paraphrase outputs, vary the amount of available text, and test generalization across prompt distributions and languages. Our results show that fingerprints persist beyond broad family differences, remaining detectable in distilled models and even LoRA adaptations of the same base model. Although attribution weakens as surface and lexical cues are altered, substantial model-specific signal remains. These signals transfer across prompt sources and languages, with semantic representations proving most robust across languages. These findings show that model identity is recoverable at multiple resolutions, but that recoverability depends on model similarity and the evidence available in the text.


### [439] [Large Flow Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28559)
*Arnav Chavan, Chanhyuk Lee, Udbhav Bamba, Jaehoon Yoo, Seunghoon Hong, Aditi Raghunathan, Jinwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Continuous flow language models offer a path to parallel generation with tools for control and acceleration unavailable to discrete diffusion, yet effective techniques for building them at the billion-parameter scale remain less established. We present Lafa (Large flow language model), a fast, high-performing flow language model scaled to 8B parameters in a data-efficient manner. Lafa is a blockwise flow model with causal attention on the prefix and bidirectional, asynchronous flow within each block. This design enables efficient training by adapting pretrained autoregressive (AR) models and fast inference via prefix caching and blockwise parallel decoding. We train Lafa at 0.5B, 7B, and 8B scales with fewer than 30B tokens, more efficient than previous diffusion models, and evaluate it on instruction following, reasoning, and coding, where the learned flow matches or surpasses the quality of AR and can be distilled into flow maps for one- and few-step generation. Notably, flow training preserves or even improves pretrained AR capabilities via a multi-token prediction effect, which we leverage in a self-speculative decoding scheme that verifies flow-based drafts autoregressively within a single model. This yields 2.54x the throughput of an AR model at comparable quality, outperforming multi-token prediction and discrete diffusion methods. Our findings show that continuous flows can serve as a scalable foundation for language modeling.


### [440] [Capable Teacher, Myopic Scorer: Reward-Free Teacher Repair for Agentic On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28586)
*Shu Zhao, Tan Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Agentic on-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student agent on its own trajectories while a teacher scores every token. A natural explanation of where it goes wrong is compounding error: the student's mistakes carry its trajectories beyond the region where the teacher is reliable, so recent methods keep training inside that region, through episode-length curricula, teacher-guided turns, prefix replay, or teacher intervention where the two disagree. We show this explanation is incomplete. From the states where the student departs most from the teacher, the teacher's own takeovers still finish the task, yet the student trained on its token scores keeps running out of turns. We attribute this to the myopia of next-token scoring. The teacher rates each token without seeing where the episode ends, so its scores let through the safe, delaying moves that outcome-based RL would punish with zero reward, a penalty agentic OPD lacks. We call this difference between what the teacher does and what it scores the rollout-scorer gap. We propose Rollout-to-Scorer Distillation (R2S), which repairs the scorer before distillation by mining high-disagreement states from the untrained student, running teacher takeovers from them, keeping the states where enough takeovers pass a reward-free check such as consensus or an LLM judge, and fine-tuning the teacher to give its takeover-informed scores on every assistant token from the divergence turn onward, without the takeover in context. The repaired teacher is frozen as the sole scorer for standard OPD, requiring no task reward during repair or training. Across three environments, two teachers and four students, R2S outperforms three state-of-the-art agentic-OPD methods in all twelve teacher-student-environment cells, by 12.8 success points on average over the best of them in each cell. Project page: this https URL .


### [441] [Benchmarking Arabic Dialects for Math Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28607)
*Noor Abo Mokh, Teresa Lynn, Nizar Habash, Ahmed Elshabrawy, Sara Alhajeri, Alaa Elsetohy, Preslav Nakov, Alham Fikri Aji*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Mathematical reasoning remains underexplored for low-resource languages, including Dialectal Arabic. To bridge this gap, we introduce the DAR-MGSM (Dialectal Arabic Multilingual Grade School Math) Benchmark, the first manually curated benchmark for evaluating mathematical reasoning in Dialectal Arabic. DAR-MGSM consists of math word problems (MWPs) requiring multi-step reasoning and covers five major Arabic dialects alongside Modern Standard Arabic (MSA). The benchmark comprises two parallel versions: translated-only and translated & culturally localized. The latter version adapts entities and references to regional varieties. We evaluate eleven Arabic-centric and multilingual large language models on DAR-MGSM. The results show that the performance generally declines on the localized version, highlighting the challenges introduced by culturally grounded adaptations. However, the magnitude of this effect varies across models, with Arabic-centric models exhibiting different levels of sensitivity to localization. A mechanistic interpretability analysis shows that translated and localized variants of the same MWPs often induce distinct internal reasoning trajectories, even when aggregate accuracy remains similar. Overall, our results show that mathematical reasoning performance depends on both linguistic variety and cultural context.


<div id='cs.IT'></div>

# cs.IT [[Back]](#toc)

### [442] [Certified Adaptive List Decoding for Error-Correcting Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27805)
*Jaeho Hwang, Byung-Sun Won, Uiwon Hwang*

Main category: cs.IT

Abstract: Maximum-likelihood (ML) decoding of linear block codes is NP-hard, so neural decoders are evaluated by how closely they approach it. We observe that proving a candidate optimal can be far easier than finding it, and build a decoder that does both. We cast decoding as a search over an absorbing-state discrete diffusion model, whose sequential commitment of coordinates turns generation into a tree, and run a probability beam search over it. A dual bound from a linear-programming relaxation and a distance-based test, both derived from the code, then certify per frame that the current candidate is the global ML solution; because both are theorems, certified frames incur no accuracy-computation trade-off. Across $15$ codes and five operating points we certify up to $99.9\%$ of frames, the two tests dividing the space as their derivations predict, one strong for sparse parity-check matrices and the other for codes with a large minimum distance. Certification also behaves as a per-frame reliability signal, with $96.7$-$100\%$ of all frame errors falling on uncertified frames. Against an exact ML reference at $5$ dB the decoder is within one standard deviation on three of the four codes where the reference is uncensored, and against four neural baselines it attains the best bit-error rate in $71$ of $75$ cells.


<div id='stat.ML'></div>

# stat.ML [[Back]](#toc)

### [443] [Dynamic Dual-Feedback Conformal Inference for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26987)
*Songlin Du, Ling Luo, Uwe Aickelin*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Quantifying uncertainty in time series forecasting is particularly demanding because sequential data exhibit temporal dependence and are prone to distributional changes. Conformal inference has emerged as a powerful uncertainty quantification approach through the construction of prediction sets. Recent advances have introduced online conformal methods that adaptively adjust prediction thresholds through feedback mechanisms. However, the existing feedback mechanism typically relies solely on miscoverage indicators (actual feedback), whether the true label falls within the interval at each time step, while overlooking the empirical prediction threshold (estimated feedback) that is derived from the oracle conformal method. In this paper, we propose Dynamic Dual-feedback Conformal Inference (DDCI), which incorporates a dual-feedback mechanism consisting of actual feedback and estimated feedback. The former drives the primary adjustment of the intervals based on true observations, while the latter dampens excessive expansions or contractions by leveraging empirical thresholds from conformal inference during updates. By balancing these two signals, DDCI achieves more stable and narrower prediction intervals in sequential settings while maintaining near-nominal coverage in practice.


### [444] [Beyond Scalar Leaderboards: Adaptive Sampling for Pareto Frontier Identification in Multi-Objective LLM Arenas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27447)
*Liang Twist Shan, Jiahao Ai, Zhimei Ren*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: The evaluation of large language models (LLMs) is inherently multi-objective, as different models may offer different tradeoffs across performance dimensions such as math, code, and writing. Because such evaluations are often based on costly and time-consuming human feedback, it is important to draw reliable conclusions using as few comparisons as possible. In this paper, we present Vector-Borda Empirical-Gap Elimination (VB-EGE), a fixed-confidence adaptive sampling algorithm for efficiently identifying models on the Pareto frontier in arena-style evaluation, where each comparison evaluates a pair of models on a prompt with respect to a selected performance dimension. VB-EGE progressively eliminates models from an active set once sufficient statistical evidence has been accumulated to certify their Pareto status. Under a multi-objective Bradley-Terry model, we show that VB-EGE returns the correct Pareto frontier with high probability and derive an instance-dependent upper bound on its sample complexity. We further establish a worst-case lower bound showing that VB-EGE is minimax-optimal up to logarithmic factors. Simulations and experiments on three real-world Arena datasets (Arena, SearchArena, and VisionArena) demonstrate VB-EGE's empirical effectiveness.


### [445] [Quadratic Sample Complexity for Strictly Replicable Realizable Learning with Unknown Marginals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26944)
*Guangjian Zhang*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Under the quantifier $\exists A\,\forall D$, where the realizable distribution and its marginal are unknown, we determine the finite-class sample-complexity exponent for strict replicability: two independent samples and one shared seed must produce identical functions. For $\varepsilon=\delta=\rho=10^{-4}$, the worst case over $|H|\le M$ is $\Theta((\log M)^2)$, without iterated logarithms. For $d\ge1$, $0<\varepsilon\le1/16$, and $0<\delta,\rho\le10^{-3}$, every randomized, possibly improper learner valid throughout an explicit anchored realizable family requires $\Omega(d^2/(\varepsilon\rho^2))$ samples. For the full Boolean class $P_d$, $d\ge2$, in the same parameter range, we prove the matching characterization $\Theta(d^2/(\varepsilon\rho^2)+\varepsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta))$ using a proper Poissonized ball-sampling algorithm. We also give an explicit proper Gibbs upper bound for every finite measurable Boolean class. Our lower bound uses square-root probabilities and volumetric packing; the Gibbs proof builds on established coupling and generalization identities. Finally, we characterize the known-marginal worst case for $P_d$, showing why that interface must be kept distinct.


### [446] [Robust Latent Variable Graphical Models: Exact Recovery and Fundamental Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27010)
*Chuansen Peng, Xiaojing Shen*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study robust estimation of a Gaussian precision matrix that decomposes as a sparse matrix minus a symmetric, possibly indefinite, low-rank matrix, a structure that arises when latent-variable marginalization is combined with conditioning on linear measurements. The proposed convex estimator replaces the sample covariance by the centered Gaussian measure-transformed (MT) covariance, which preserves the signed graph of the sparse component under the nominal model, and penalizes the low-rank component by the nuclear norm of a congruence transform, an atomic norm that encodes structural side information. Our main result is a primal--dual witness theorem: under restricted invertibility and irrepresentability conditions on the Fisher information, the raw convex optimizer is unique, has no false edges, recovers the signs of all sufficiently strong edges, and has exactly the true rank and inertia. These conditions reduce to the graphical-lasso conditions when the low-rank part is absent, and we verify them from primitive incoherence and near-isotropy assumptions. Over a class on which they hold uniformly, we prove information-theoretic limits for Frobenius estimation, signed graph recovery, and inertia recovery. In particular, inertia recovery requires $N\gtrsim p/\nu_{\min}^2$ samples, where $\nu_{\min}$ is the smallest nonzero eigenvalue modulus of the low-rank part; the spectral beta-min condition of the witness theorem is necessary up to a $\sqrt{\log p}$ factor; and the Frobenius risk attains the minimax rate up to a factor $\sqrt{\mu(1+\min\{d,r\})\log p}$, where $\mu$ is the incoherence parameter, $d$ the maximal degree, and $r$ the rank. We further quantify robustness under Huber contamination, with a bias bound that is sharp for the MT functional and vanishes for remote outliers, and give a globally convergent linearized ADMM with closed-form updates.


### [447] [Gaussian Approximation of Besov Functions for Logistic Classification on Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27022)
*Guanhang Lei, Lei Shi, Xiaoming Yuan*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study Gaussian kernel approximation and regularized logistic classification on a connected compact smooth embedded manifold $X\subset\mathbb R^d$ without boundary and of intrinsic dimension $\varrho$. Two main challenges are to derive a fast oracle inequality without assuming a bounded Bayes function and to construct Gaussian approximants that exploit both the intrinsic dimension and the target's regularity without saturation. We address the first challenge through a Bernstein-type inequality whose constant depends linearly on the supremum norm of the predictor. Combined with localized complexity estimates, this yields an oracle inequality over separable normed function spaces. To address the second challenge, we establish a uniform Sobolev operator expansion and cancel its lower-order bias terms using a signed combination of Gaussian kernel integral operators at different bandwidths. Real interpolation then gives an $L_2(X)$ approximation error of order $\gamma^\alpha$ for functions in $B^\alpha_{2,\infty}(X)$, where $\gamma$ is the bandwidth. By constructing the approximants directly on the manifold without ambient extension, we obtain a squared Gaussian RKHS norm bound of order $\gamma^{-\varrho}$. This construction thus overcomes second-order saturation while retaining norm control governed by the intrinsic dimension. When the marginal distribution has a density $g\in L_p(X)$, these results yield the logistic excess-risk rate $\bigl((\log n)^{\varrho+1}/n\bigr)^{4\alpha(1-1/p)/(4\alpha(1-1/p)+3\varrho)}$. We further show that clipping predictions yields the faster clipped logistic excess-risk rate $\bigl((\log n)^{\varrho+2}/n\bigr)^{2\alpha(1-1/p)/(2\alpha(1-1/p)+\varrho)}$. For bounded densities, its polynomial exponent matches that of the minimax optimal rate for squared-loss regression, and its corresponding excess misclassification risk also matches the minimax optimal rate.


### [448] [Diffusion Score Estimation with Shallow Neural Networks: Regularity, Approximation, and Finite-Sample Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27206)
*Fan Liu, Yahong Yang, Wei Zhu*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of estimating Ornstein--Uhlenbeck diffusion scores in $\mathbb R^d$ using denoising score matching (DSM) with finite-width shallow ReLU networks, and its implications for reverse-process sampling. Under weak differentiability and polynomial-growth conditions on normalized derivatives of the initial density, we combine complementary estimates from the Gaussian transition kernel and the initial density to obtain explicit Sobolev bounds that are uniform in time on each fixed ball. Combining these bounds with existing shallow-network approximation theory gives explicit control of the approximation error and a sufficient path-norm budget. We then use truncation and localized Rademacher complexity to establish high-probability risk bounds for path-norm-constrained empirical DSM minimizers with unbounded inputs and labels. Under additional bounded-density and sub-Gaussian assumptions, suitable choices of network width and path-norm budget yield squared $L^2(P_t)$ score risk $\widetilde O(n^{-(d+3)/(2d+3)})$ at each fixed time $t>0$, where $n$ is the number of independent training samples and $P_t$ is the forward marginal. The nonasymptotic bounds retain explicit dependence on diffusion time and the regularity and tail parameters of the initial distribution. Aggregating the score errors over a time grid and applying existing sampling theory together with an early-stopping bound yields a high-probability total-variation error $\widetilde O(n^{-(d+3)/(4d+6)})$ between the generated and original data distributions under suitable sampling parameters. Here $\widetilde O$ suppresses logarithmic factors for fixed dimension, confidence level, and initial distribution.


### [449] [Communication Complexity of Universal Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27402)
*Lunjia Hu, Anish Jayant, Peter Li, Princewill Okoroafor, Abhishek Shetty*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Motivated by the notion of features from machine learning, we study the complexity of representing data that allows for evaluation with a given family of functions. Concretely, we consider a deterministic encoder that observes $X\sim D$ and produces a finite encoding before a task $f\in\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{X}}$ is specified. For each task, a decoder must predict $f(X)$ with error at most $\varepsilon$ under $D$. We construct an encoder that outputs a representation with $O(d^{*}\log(1/\varepsilon))$ bits, where $d^{*}$ is the dual VC dimension of $\mathcal{F}$. Our encoder greedily selects $O((d^{*}/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))$ functions from the class and uses their joint label pattern to produce the encoding. The key idea in our upper bound is a novel potential function argument, where we compare the entropy gained from each greedy selection with the number of patterns allowed by the dual VC dimension. We present lower bounds showing that the representation length achieved by our encoder is worst-case optimal for every $d^* \ge 1$ and $\varepsilon < 1/4$ up to a universal constant factor, and instance-wise optimal for every family $\mathcal{F}$ up to an $O(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ factor. We give two ways to learn the representation from unlabeled samples, with different guarantees on the representation size and sample complexity. Experimentally, we verify the greedy construction's accuracy-size tradeoff and explore its connection to neural sparse autoencoders.


### [450] [What Moved the Alarm Score? Causal Attribution with Exact Computation and Scarce-Incident Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27534)
*Masaaki Takada, Ryusei Shingaki*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Alarms in industrial systems and scientific instruments often demand immediate diagnosis despite scarce incident data. To guide intervention, an explanation must trace how changes in the data-generating process propagate through the causal structure to alter the alarm score, rather than merely flagging shifts that may be downstream effects. Yet exact attribution across interacting causal components appears combinatorial, while existing methods often either assume an unchanged causal model or require enough incident data to estimate the changed system reliably. We address this setting by formulating the alarm-score change as a Shapley game over hybrid structural causal model (SCM) states. Treating structural functions and exogenous laws as distinct players yields a finer-grained, function/law-resolved report of how each type of change propagates to the alarm score. The same framework defines realized-event and population reports, with an event--population coherence identity linking their attributions. For ordered linear SCMs and quadratic scores, a causal first- and second-moment recursion combined with quadrature computes the full Shapley vector exactly in polynomial time, eliminating combinatorial computation. To estimate this target from scarce incident data, we adapt a well-observed reference SCM to the incident regime using cross-fitted estimates of mechanism changes and residual moments, and derive attribution-error bounds and top-k recovery guarantees. Experiments on controlled and measured shifts illustrate the diagnoses and assess scaling to hundreds of variables and estimation from small incident samples.


### [451] [Bayesian Neural Networks via Dirichlet Process Priors on the Data-Generating Distribution for Randomized Value Functions in Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27570)
*Sumit Vashishtha*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Bayesian neural networks (BNNs) offer a principled framework for quantifying epistemic uncertainty. However, standard weight-space priors yield complex, high-dimensional neural network posteriors that are computationally prohibitive to sample from, and popular approximate inference schemes often fail to capture epistemic uncertainty effectively---a failure mode particularly acute in reinforcement learning (RL) \citep{osband2018randomized}. To resolve this challenge, we introduce a new class of BNNs that quantify uncertainty by placing Bayesian nonparametric Dirichlet Process (DP) priors directly on the underlying data-generating distribution rather than on network parameters. Building on this foundation, we develop DP-DQN, a reinforcement learning algorithm that leverages DP-BNNs to maintain posterior distributions over value functions and perform efficient Thompson sampling. By grounding randomized value functions in Bayesian nonparametrics, DP-DQN unifies data-driven posterior reweighting (via the Bayesian Bootstrap) with generative prior hypotheses injected directly through the DP base measure, enabling decisive ``deep exploration'' with a single neural network without the overhead of multi-network ensembles.


### [452] [Ask to Learn: Query-Based Collaborative Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27606)
*Kaicheng Zhang, Hongqian Niu, Laixi Shi, Didong Li, Guannan Qu, Zhun Deng*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning is fundamentally limited by the coverage of the available data, while collecting additional trajectories through online exploration may be costly, unsafe, or infeasible. We study whether this limitation can be overcome without directly interacting with the underlying environment. Motivated by practical settings such as collaborator-mediated simulation, where a collaborator can provide information complementary to the offline data, we introduce query-based collaborative reinforcement learning to study how to acquire this information efficiently. The learner starts with a fixed offline dataset and adaptively queries state-time pairs; the collaborator samples an action according to a fixed, possibly suboptimal policy, and the simulator returns the corresponding reward and next state. For finite-horizon tabular problems, we derive an instance-dependent information-theoretic lower bound on the additional queries required to identify an $\epsilon$-optimal policy and develop a local quadratic approximation. Guided by this characterization, we develop an adaptive query-allocation algorithm whose query complexity nearly matches the local benchmark under suitable conditions. Experiments on multiple synthetic and semi-synthetic environments demonstrate the empirical efficiency of the proposed method.


### [453] [Annealed correlation and quenched noise sensitivity in biased deep neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27983)
*Johan Jonasson*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: In earlier work by Jonasson, Steif and Zetterqvist (2023), fully connected deep neural networks with layers of width $n$, i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian weights, sign activations and no bias terms were shown to be annealed and quenched noise sensitive with respect to any joint probability distribution $\mathbb{Q}^{(n)}$ on the input $\boldsymbol{\omega}$ and its perturbation $\boldsymbol{\eta}$ satisfying a mild positive-correlation condition and $\mathbb{Q}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\eta} \in \{\boldsymbol{\omega},-\boldsymbol{\omega}\}) \to 0$. Here we add independent Gaussian biases of the natural order and show that quenched noise sensitivity is retained over a broad range of depths, indeed under the slightly weaker assumption that $\mathbb{Q}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\omega} = \boldsymbol{\eta}) \to 0$. Thus, subject to the positive-correlation condition, quenched noise sensitivity holds under the minimal requirement that the perturbed input is asymptotically unlikely to coincide with the original input. At the same time, the annealed covariance remains bounded away from zero and is entirely accounted for by fluctuations in the network-dependent output means.


### [454] [Near-Optimal Regret Bounds for UCB-Style Gaussian Process Bandits via Data Thinning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28136)
*Shion Takeno, Shogo Iwazaki, Ichiro Takeuchi*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Gaussian process upper confidence bound (GP-UCB) has been widely studied, though its known regret upper bound is suboptimal. To obtain near-optimal regret upper bounds, existing studies mainly focus on non-adaptive methods, such as uncertainty sampling, whose practical effectiveness is often limited. This paper shows that, under a Gaussian noise assumption, UCB-style algorithms can achieve near-optimal regret upper bounds via data thinning, a statistical technique for creating independent datasets from a single dataset. First, we show that data thinning can yield tighter confidence intervals in the bandit setting, and that a direct extension of GP-UCB achieves a near-optimal simple regret upper bound. Furthermore, by leveraging the tighter confidence intervals and combining GP-UCB with a phased elimination algorithm, we show a near-optimal cumulative regret upper bound. Finally, we demonstrate the effectiveness of UCB-style algorithms incorporating data thinning via numerical experiments.


### [455] [Predicting silent failures of learned inference under restricted access: what measurement physics and prior information explain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28155)
*Mario Gianni*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Learned priors are used to reconstruct regions that measurements barely reach, and to report uncertainty there. They can fail silently: the reported interval excludes the truth while every ground-truth-free diagnostic stays within its noise. We call such errors the blind set and ask whether they can be predicted before the target data are seen. For a correctly specified linear-Gaussian model averaged over the prior, the risk of a silent failure factorises exactly into non-detection and non-coverage, which does not hold for learned systems in general; we build a score that approximates this reference by linearising a VAE prior. In a prospective study on real X-ray CT objects (2DeteCT) under simulated restricted access, with predictions committed by hash before any trial, the score ranked the realised blindness of two learned inference systems (latent Laplace and ensemble Kalman inversion) with Spearman $\rho = 0.79$ and 0.84 over 240 pre-specified errors. Simple structural predictors would also have passed these tests. Sequential post-hoc analyses, each specified before its own computation, show that under bounded reach the learned score gave no detectable improvement over a physics-based baseline, in rankings or trial-level log scores. At the weakest attenuation studied, prior information improved rankings over the physics-based baseline, most clearly for ensemble Kalman inversion, but a Gaussian prior did as well and the gain was not detectable at the trial level. The score is not a calibrated probability. Silent failures can be anticipated here, but their structure follows mainly from measurement access and the decision quantity rather than from the learned prior.


### [456] [Geometry-Aware Block Selection for Sparse Variational Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28256)
*Matteo Hoxha*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Sparse variational Gaussian processes (SVGPs) are a widely used, principled approach to scalable Gaussian process modelling. Their success stems from their theoretically interpretable structure: they provide a lower bound on the marginal likelihood of the full GP while allowing scal- able stochastic optimization. Recent work has explored modifications of the posterior conditional distribution and has shown that such extensions can improve upon this well-established framework. In particular, introducing a block-diagonal structured scal- ing matrix leads to a tighter lower bound on the GP marginal likelihood. We investigate whether selecting blocks that tighten the variational objective improves test RMSE and NLL at a fixed inducing-point budget. Since the scaling matrix contributes to the variational lower bound through an upper bound on the log-determinant of the full residual covariance matrix, our work makes the following contributions. First, we introduce a simple, scalable, geometry-aware algorithm, with explicit computational complexity and low computational overhead, that recovers a large fraction of this log- determinant contribution by selecting an appropriate partition of the data. Second, we show how to train these models using a doubly stochastic optimization framework. Finally, we investigate how sparse GP methods based on alternative conditional posterior structures can be used to learn better hyperparameters and inducing points for SVGP.


### [457] [Debiased Prior-Data Fitted Networks for Amortized Bayesian Semi-Parametric Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28314)
*Valentyn Melnychuk, Emil Javurek, Stefan Feuerriegel*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) have recently gained popularity as an end-to-end approach to amortized Bayesian inference. Yet, their statistical behavior for semi-parametric targets, such as means under missingness or causal effects, remains poorly understood. In this work, we develop theoretical foundations for debiased PFNs to perform inference on finite-dimensional targets in general semi-parametric settings. First, we show that, despite being Bayes-optimal with respect to the target, an idealized PFN can exhibit a posterior concentration rate that implicitly depends on the concentration rates of (latent) nuisance functions and can become slower as the prior becomes more diverse. Then, to speed up the target posterior concentration rates, we introduce two generic debiasing strategies based on efficient influence functions: (i) a label correction and (ii) a prior correction. Finally, we show that the debiased PFNs (i) & (ii) possess concentration rates that asymptotically match a convergence rate given by traditional frequentist bias-corrected estimators. In this way, we establish a novel connection between amortized Bayesian inference with PFNs and frequentist semi-parametric efficiency theory. Under additional assumptions, this allows us to guarantee local and global optimality of PFN-based estimators (namely, the Bernstein-von Mises property and minimax optimality, respectively). Therefore, our work provides guidelines for designing debiased PFNs for semi-parametric inference with principled and provable asymptotic guarantees.


### [458] [Confessions of a Masked MDP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28360)
*Sebastian Reboul, Lorenzo Croissant, Hélène Halconruy*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, one presumes the action set to be known ahead of time, and, thus, one must know if any state-action pairs are not permissible ahead of computations. In some applications, determining this action mask is the main computational bottleneck. Isolating this problem to highlight its core properties, we study how to combine the search for an optimal policy with the learning of the mask itself, under a linear MDP model in which each attempted action reveals the exact value of a linear score determining its validity. In this framework, we first construct an algorithm based on deterministic optimistic optimization and show that it is minimax optimal, which we show is also computationally efficient. However, it is sub-optimal on non-degenerate instances, as we show. We propose an alternative, which is also computationally efficient, which plans its exploration strategy. This algorithm admits a formal interpretation as an analogue of information-directed-sampling for deterministic feedback, which may be of independent interest. Finally, we study an application of this framework to transfer learning, where the invalid action set changes over time.


<div id='physics.comp-ph'></div>

# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [459] [FoundPDE: A generative foundation model for data-driven symbolic discovery of partial differential equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26963)
*Langchen Liu, Junlin Luo, Min Zhu, Zhuoran Yang, Lu Lu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

Abstract: Discovering governing partial differential equations (PDEs) from observed data is a central inverse problem in computational science and engineering: the goal is not only to predict trajectories, but to identify interpretable symbolic laws that can be analyzed and reused. Existing PDE discovery methods often depend on hand-designed libraries, accurate numerical derivatives, or per-equation symbolic search, making them fragile under sparse observations, noisy data, broad equation families, and unseen symbolic compositions. Here, we introduce FoundPDE, a pretrained data-to-symbol foundation model for PDE discovery. FoundPDE maps discretized spatio-temporal trajectories to symbolic equations with a transformer, uses Fourier and first-order spatial-derivative augmentation to expose complementary trajectory information, refines numerical coefficients, and supports downstream extension with improved generalization through few-shot fine-tuning and hierarchical discovery. On a clean in-domain benchmark spanning 20 PDE families, our model reaches 97.50% type recognition. Under coefficient shifts beyond the training support, type recognition remains 90.53%; under multiplicative Gaussian noise on the model input, it is 94.43% at a 2% noise level but falls to 52.09% at 10%. For unseen composite equations, few-shot fine-tuning reaches over 90% exact recovery on four held-out families, and we further develop a hierarchical discovery algorithm that recovers two-term composites without fine-tuning FoundPDE. By amortizing symbolic discovery across equation families, FoundPDE shifts PDE discovery from per-dataset search toward a reusable pretrained model that extends to new physics with limited additional data, a step toward foundation models that uncover interpretable governing laws directly from scientific data.


<div id='astro-ph.IM'></div>

# astro-ph.IM [[Back]](#toc)

### [460] [Graph Neural Networks for Astrophysics and Cosmology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27051)
*Christian Kragh Jespersen*

Main category: astro-ph.IM

Abstract: Physics seldom makes breakthroughs when we consider objects only in isolation. Instead we model and measure how objects sit among their neighbours, spatial, temporal, or otherwise: a galaxy in its environment, a halo in its history, a photon hit in a detector. Graphs are ideally suited for encoding exactly these relations, and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) learn the geometric functions that respect them. This review presents the use of GNNs for astrophysics and cosmology, and argues that GNNs often suit physics and physical data better than any alternative: they fit the native geometry of the domain and the signal, enable the incorporation of the symmetries and prior knowledge we already trust and have toiled to build, and stay flexible enough to capture what we do not yet understand. GNNs' physically intuitive workings also render them more interpretable than other deep-learning architectures. We show how we can begin from general principles of the geometry of deep learning and build the graph and the graph neural network from the ground up, and how other architectures, like the convolutional network and the transformer, can be seen as special cases of one unified geometric blueprint. We survey applications across cosmology, galaxy formation, dynamics, and instrumentation; and we chart the frontier. From this survey, we find that only one in six articles outside instrumentation applies GNNs to data rather than just simulations. We also distil a set of best practices, including when not to reach for a graph. Throughout, we will prioritise physical intuition and foundational principles over mathematical machinery whenever possible.


### [461] [A Radiation-Hardened, Fault-Aware Quantized Neural Network That Self-Heals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27621)
*Peter Xiangyuan Ma, Joshua S. Bloom*

Main category: astro-ph.IM

Abstract: Deep learning (DL) models are increasingly being deployed into radiation-challenging environments in spacecraft, particle accelerators, and, soon, space-based data centers. Instantiated as a learned orchestration of numbers stored in persistent memory, models are susceptible to continual degradation as high-energy particles corrupt data. At inference time, new data passed through the network fundamentally break the off-line assumptions and guarantees of a static model determined during training. To preserve the quality and efficacy of the models, fault tolerance against bit flips is a clear objective. In this work we developed a runtime method for repairing the quantized weights of neural networks (NNs) after bit flips on commodity, commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) hardware, without storing extra replicas. Our work extends the well-established Fault-Aware Training (FAT) paradigm by adding an alignment penalty to concentrate undamaged activations and weights into a low-rank subspace, and we then reconstruct flagged corrupted coordinates from the uncorrupted survivors. We call this method {\it Projection Healing} and apply it to six regression benchmarks on quantized NNs. Holding a FAT checkpoint fixed and toggling only the runtime heal gives a further ${\sim}23\%$ reduction in the native-unit excess integral. Across those datasets the method extends the operational scrub interval by a median of $2.04\times$ at a $+5\%$ RMSE degradation and $1.96\times$ at $+50\%$, compared with $1.62\times$ and $1.07\times$ for classical Single Error Correction, Double Error Detection (SECDED). This introduces a new path towards designing resilient data-driven models, complementing established hardware redundancy, ECC, and FAT-based techniques.


<div id='cs.DB'></div>

# cs.DB [[Back]](#toc)

### [462] [VS-Flash: Harnessing Vector Search with Computational Memory-Semantic Flash](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26960)
*Donguk Kim, Sangjun Pae, Daeyoung Lee, Dong Kim*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: As generative AI continues to evolve, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems are driving rapid growth in vector database capacity requirements. As datasets outgrow host DRAM, vector databases rely on MMap-based serving over SSDs to extend capacity. However, high-fan-out ANN searches generate concurrent irregular accesses that contend for host memory and storage resources, degrading bulk throughput and inflating tail latency under mixed workloads. In this paper, we address these challenges through memory-semantic capacity and workload-aware compute placement. VS-Flash exposes flash as a large-capacity memory-semantic space and dynamically selects the execution engine according to runtime query and workload characteristics. It offloads throughput-oriented large-batch queries to a Computational Memory-Semantic Flash (CMS-Flash) device, while executing latency-sensitive small-batch queries on the host CPU. Both paths access the same resident segments, avoiding a separate host-DRAM replica. Across three billion-scale datasets, VS-Flash improves bulk-search throughput over vanilla Milvus by 36.8X on average, with up to a 57.4X improvement. Under mixed workloads, VS-Flash reduces the p99 latency of latency-sensitive requests by up to 24.8X while sustaining device-side bulk search. These results show that VS-Flash delivers scalable vector search beyond host DRAM capacity while jointly improving bulk throughput and small-request tail latency.


### [463] [SweepANN: A GPU-Accelerated Large-K Approximate Vector Search for Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27306)
*Zhenyu Zhang, Qi Lin, Nan Zhang, Zhichao Cao*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: DrugCLIP places small molecules and protein pockets in one space, turning genome-wide drug discovery into large-K retrieval by vector proximity. A campaign fixes over 20K pockets in advance for one library of over 500M molecules. Each pocket keeps K = 500K candidates, 0.1% of the collection, versus 100 in ANN benchmarks. Missed hits never get tested, so mean recall must reach 99% relative to exact search. Large answers make IVF probe every list holding a result and graph search track K or more candidates, so costs grow with K. On our CPUs and GPUs, neither index family reaches the target without losing most of its edge over scanning. SweepANN partitions compressed vectors across GPUs and scans each resident partition. One matrix multiplication scores each block for a whole query batch, reading it once. Several plans perform the K-dependent selection and merging of each query's K answers, each returning an exact top-K under compressed scoring. The window plan retains each query's best K from sampled blocks as its seed, and only rows above each seed's minimum score, shared once across GPUs, enter the buffers. Four Blackwell GPUs (RTX PRO 6000) answer all 26,562 pockets with a resident time of about 61 seconds, excluding library loading, and 99.6% mean recall relative to exact FP32 search. DrugCLIP reports roughly 24 hours with eight A100 GPUs in its own campaign. On 500M rows, the window plan runs 1.41x faster than SweepANN's grouped plan in the same memory. Across 25M-500M rows and K of 10K-500K, all GPU IVF configurations meeting the recall target take 8.2-24x SweepANN's resident time. On DEEP1B's 1B vectors across eight GPUs, against SweepANN's exact full-score scan of uncompressed FP32 rows, the window plan cuts resident time by 5.4x and 3.2x at K = 100K and 500K. It also cuts per-GPU peak memory by 6.0x and 5.3x, losing 1.0 and 0.7 mean-recall points.


### [464] [MultiCard: Multiple Attribute Cardinality Estimation via Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27019)
*Mobina Koochaknia, Maryam Ramezani, Mohammad Hasan Shafaat*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: Cardinality estimation is essential for query optimization, but most existing learned estimators focus on a single predicate type, such as string LIKE clauses or numerical range filters. In practice, analytical workloads often combine both types, while the accuracy of string estimators tends to decrease with longer patterns. We introduce MultiCard, a comprehensive learned cardinality estimator that unifies LIKE predicates and numerical filters within a single end-to-end architecture. By combining a Transformer for long-range sparse patterns with an LSTM for short, frequent patterns, MultiCard achieves robust pattern modeling. In parallel, a numerical encoder integrates catalog histograms and structural predicate features, enabling the model to dynamically assess histogram estimation accuracy across different operators and data skew conditions. A final Transformer layer captures interactions among heterogeneous predicates before a multilayer perceptron predicts query cardinalities. We also introduce UPCI, a workload generator that produces length-balanced LIKE patterns, multi-column numerical predicates, and exact cardinality labels for training and evaluation. Experiments on five real-world datasets show that, for string-only workloads, MultiCard achieves the best or near-best Q-error across all datasets compared with state-of-the-art methods. For mixed multi-attribute queries, MultiCard reduces PostgreSQL's mean Q-error by up to 16 times while maintaining an inference latency below 5 ms.


### [465] [Local Inference of LLMs on Long Contexts: A Data Systems Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28223)
*Mattias Dürrmeier, Alberto Lerner*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: Large language models can solve complex problems across a variety of subjects, such as mathematics and coding. For such complex analytical tasks, models with a long context perform better. Serving models with long context is currently mostly done on powerful systems with multiple GPUs, which are too expensive for most users. An alternative is local inference, or serving a model on a user's own machine, which removes per-token costs and rate limits, besides keeping sensitive data private and giving users full control over the models they run. The common understanding, however, is that local LLMs need compression (e.g., quantization) to run on a single GPU. In this paper, we set out to study the limitations that prevent serving long-context models for single users, besides the capacity issue. Rather than characterizing a single model on one GPU, we analyze how different models behave across several GPU generations. This lets us uncover not just individual bottlenecks but broader trends in where inefficiencies arise. Our analysis confirms that, as expected, self-attention dominates the runtime for long-context inference. More unexpectedly, our evaluation reveals that the current structure of self-attention leaves key GPU resources significantly underutilized, and the newest GPU is often the least well served. Many of these findings are fundamentally data management problems, from paging the KV cache to improving cache utilization and synchronization in the GPU, which is why we bring this study to the Data Systems community. To engage this community, we present our findings as a series of experimental observations and formulate recommendations for future work.


<div id='math.NA'></div>

# math.NA [[Back]](#toc)

### [466] [Low Residuals, Wrong Value: Learning the Viscosity Solution of Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs Equations with PINNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27193)
*Raul Jimenez, Pablo Tejerina-Pérez, Leonid Sarieddine, Pedro Tarancón-Álvarez, Marisol Traforetti, Pau Solé-Vilaró, Oleksii Ignatenko, Nazar Tkach, Pavlos Protopapas*

Main category: math.NA

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) and other residual-trained solvers judge a candidate solution by the size of its sampled PDE residual. For first-order Hamilton-Jacobi equations this is not sufficient: the same equation and boundary data admit Lipschitz almost-everywhere solutions that differ from the viscosity solution by $O(1)$ while violating the equation only on sets of measure zero. We study whether residual minimization selects such solutions in a fixed-horizon pursuit-evasion game whose Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs value function is known in closed form on the circle $S^1$ and on the flat tori $\mathbb{T}^{2}$ and $\mathbb{T}^{4}$. We train four neural parameterizations with ten seeds on each geometry, 120 runs in total. A standard network with periodic Fourier features converges to folded almost-everywhere solutions in all 30 runs: small residuals and the expected capture-layer gradient, yet values that are wrong over most of the domain. A network with periodicity imposed through the loss recovers the viscosity solution in one and two dimensions but becomes unreliable in four. Two parameterizations that receive the geodesic distance do substantially better: a single network fed the wrapped displacement and its distance recovers the viscosity solution in all 30 runs, while a minimum over periodic image charts succeeds in 27 of 30 and introduces a wrong-image failure mode. Finally, for the convex Hamiltonian considered here, we use the semiconcavity of the viscosity solution to build a mesh-free finite-difference test that needs only forward network evaluations; it detects the folded solutions and the harmful min-of-charts failures without access to the exact value function.
